<meta>This page continues the alchemical dream vision. Having previously meditated on Moses and the Golden Calf, the narrator now interacts directly with King Solomon, who personifies the height of earthly and divine wisdom. The "naked maiden" introduced here is a common alchemical trope for the "Materia Prima" (the raw starting material), which appears worthless or "dirty" to the ignorant but contains the seed of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

...to explain categorically, Solomon turned back, stared at me, and spoke: 
> I am the wisest on Earth; beautiful and delightful is my harem, and the magnificence of my queens surpasses the gold of Ophir. The adornment of my concubines overshadows the rays of the sun, and the beauty of my virgins outshines the moonlight. So heavenly are my ladies, so unfathomable my wisdom, and so unsearchable my understanding. 

Then I answered and bowed, half-terrified: 
> Behold, if I have found favor in your sight, because I am poor, give me this naked maiden whom I have chosen from among all others for the preservation of my life. Her clothes are indeed stained and torn, but I will cleanse her and love her from my heart. She shall be my sister, my bride, because with one of her eyes and one of her necklaces, she has captured my heart and made me so fervent that I lie sick with love. 
<note>The narrator uses the language of the biblical Song of Solomon to describe his desire for the "maiden," representing the alchemical "Secret of the Wise."</note>

And immediately Solomon gave her to me, and there was such a commotion in the harem that I woke from it and did not know what had happened to me. Nevertheless, I considered it a dream and had all sorts of subtle thoughts about it until morning. But when I stood up and performed my prayer, behold: there I saw the naked maiden’s clothes lying before my bed, but she was not there. I began to grow anxious and fearful, and all my hair stood on end, so that cold sweat ran down my whole body. Yet I took heart, recalled my dream, and meditated upon it in the fear of the Lord. However, my thoughts were unable to fathom it; for these reasons, I did not dare to inspect the clothes, much less recognize anything in them. Instead, I changed my bedchamber and, out of pure ignorance, let the clothes lie there for a long time, thinking that if I were to touch or turn them, something remarkable might happen to me. But in my sleep, I had been heavily poisoned and infected by the stench of the clothes, so that my eyes could not see the time of grace, nor could my heart recognize the great wisdom of Solomon. 

After the aforementioned clothes had lain in my bedchamber for five years and I did not know what use they were, I finally thought to burn them to clear the room. And as I spent the whole day occupied with such thoughts, the old hundred-year-old woman appeared to me the following night in a dream and spoke to me very harshly in this manner: 
> You ungrateful human! For five years now, I have entrusted to you my daughter’s clothes, among which her most noble jewels are hidden, and in all this time you have neither cleaned them nor aired them out for the sake of the moths and worms. And on top of all that, you now finally want to burn the clothes with fire? Is it not enough that you are a cause of the death and destruction of my daughter? 

Then my forehead grew somewhat hot, and I gave her this answer: 
> How should I understand this? Do you wish to make a murderer out of me? In five years, I have not seen your daughter with my eyes, nor heard the least bit from her. How then can I be a cause of her death? 

But she would not let me finish speaking, and instead said: 
> It is all true, but you have sinned against God; therefore, my daughter could not be granted to you, nor could the <term>Philosophical Lye</term> <note>original: "Philosophische Lauge"; a symbolic cleaning agent or solvent used in the early stages of alchemical purification</note> I promised follow for washing and cleaning her clothes. For when you began—when Solomon willingly gave you my daughter—and you felt a revulsion for her clothes, the planet <term>Saturn</term> <note>In alchemy, Saturn represents lead, darkness, decay, and the beginning of the "Great Work."</note>, who is her grandfather, grew angry and out of pure wrath transformed her back into what she had been before her birth. Thus, through your disdain, you have angered Saturn and caused her death, decay, and final destruction. For she is indeed the one of whom the <term>Senior</term> <note>original: "Senior"; refers to Zadith Senior, a 10th-century Arabian alchemist whose works were highly influential in Europe.</note> says: "Alas! Bring me a naked woman, for unsightly was my first body, and I had never yet become a mother until I was born a second time. Then I brought forth the power of all the roots of herbs; in my essence, I became victorious," etc. 

Such and similar heart-wrenching words seemed very strange to me, yet I restrained my temper as much as was humanly possible. Nevertheless, I solemnly protested that I knew nothing of her daughter, much less of her death, decay, and destruction. To be sure, I had kept her clothes in my bedchamber for five years, but because of my great blindness, I had not recognized them, nor could I conceive of their use; therefore, I was innocent before God and everyone. This rightful and well-founded excuse did not please the little old mother poorly; she looked at me and said: 
> I perceive and notice from your sincere conscience that you are innocent, and your innocence shall be richly and well rewarded. Therefore, out of a good heart and in secret, I want to reveal to you: that my daughter, out of special love and the affection she bore for you, left you a small, gray, <term>normalized</term> <note>Likely referring to a specific geometric or standard alchemical shape.</note> box as an inheritance among her remaining clothes, which is covered with a coarse, black, filthy lining. (And with that, she gave me a glass of lye and continued speaking.) You shall clean that same box thoroughly of the stench and filth it received from the clothes. Inside it, you will find two things: A white, silver <gloss>little boy</gloss> full of magnificent diamonds, ground and polished upon lead...

<column-break/>

...as well as a golden piece adorned with precious <term>Solar Rubies</term> <note>Refers to the red stage of the alchemical process, associated with Gold and the Sun.</note>. And this is the treasure and the entire estate of my late daughter, all of which she bequeathed and left to you as an inheritance before her transformation and passing. If you will hereafter combine this treasure together, purify it to the highest degree, and—silently yet with great patience—lock it in a warm, hidden, steamy, transparent, and moist cellar, and protect it from frost, wind, hail, sudden lightning, hot thunderbolts, and other outward destruction until the <term>White Harvest</term> <note>The "Albedo" or whitening stage of alchemy, signaling success in the first half of the process.</note>, only then will you truly experience and partake in the great glory of the inheritance. 

And at that point, I woke up again, and behold, I called upon God anxiously, asking that He might open my understanding to find the little box that had been promised and pledged to me in the dream. After finishing my prayer, I searched with the greatest diligence and desire among the clothes and found it. But the lining was so tightly closed around it and grown into it by nature that I was unable to get it off; for it would neither be cleaned with the lye nor allow itself to be split with iron, steel, or any other metal. So I let it stand again and did not know what to do with it; I considered it a work of sorcery and thought of the saying of the Prophet: 

<margin>Jeremiah 2:22</margin>
> For though you wash yourself with lye and use much soap, yet your iniquity is marked before Me, says the Lord God. 

And another year passed, during which I still did not know how to remove the lining despite my speculation and diligent reflection, until I finally went for a walk in a garden to drive away my melancholic thoughts. After walking for a long time, I sat down on a flint stone and fell fast asleep. 
<margin>Song of Solomon 5:2</margin>
I slept, but my heart was awake. Then the hundred-year-old governess appeared to me once more and said: 
> Have you received my daughter’s estate? 

I answered with a sad voice: No, I have indeed found the box, but separating the lining from it is still impossible for me at this time; the lye you gave me will not attack the lining. At this simple speech of mine, the old woman began to laugh and said: 
> Do you want to eat mussels and crabs with their shells? Must they not first be ripened and prepared by the ancient cook <term>Planet Vulcan</term> <note>Vulcan represents alchemical fire and the furnace.</note>? I told you that you should clean the gray box to the highest degree with the lye provided, as the lye originated from it—not the outward, raw lining. Instead, you must first burn the lining with the <term>White Fire</term>; only then will it be suitable. 

And immediately after this, she gave me some glowing coals wrapped in white <term>silk tinder</term>, with further instruction and indication that I should make from them a philosophical and quite artistic fire and burn the lining, and then I would soon find the gray box. And instantly a <margin>Song of Solomon 4:16</margin> north and south wind arose and both blew through the garden at once. From this I awoke, rubbed the sleep from my eyes, and noticed that the glowing coals wrapped in white silk lay at my feet. I seized them hastily and with joy, prayed diligently, called upon God, studied and labored day and night, and in the meantime thought of the magnificent and excellent saying of the philosophers, where they say: 
> Fire and <term>Azoth</term> are sufficient for you. <note>original: "Ignis & azoth tibi sufficiunt." Azoth is the "universal solvent" or "mercury of the wise" in alchemy.</note> 

Of this, Esdras also says in the fourth book: 
<margin>2 Esdras 14:39</margin>
> And he gave me a cup full of fire, and its appearance was like fire, and I drank it, and wisdom grew in me: And God gave me the fifth understanding, and my spirit was preserved in my memory, and my mouth was opened and no longer closed. And when forty nights were over, 204 books had been prepared, 70 of them for the wisest alone, and they were worthy to be read and were written on boxwood. 

And so I proceeded in silence and hope, as the little old mother had revealed to me in the dream, until a long time after the promise of Solomon, my understanding became silver and my memory became golden. But after I had properly and quite artistically set and locked away the treasure of her daughter according to the instruction and teaching of the old governess—namely: the magnificent, shining <term>Lunar Diamonds</term> and the <term>Solar Rubies</term>, both of which sprang from one box and were found in one landscape—I heard the voice of Solomon, which said: 

<margin>Song of Solomon 5:10–</margin>
> My beloved is white and ruddy, the chiefest among ten thousand. His locks are curly and black as a raven. His eyes are like the eyes of doves by the rivers of waters, washed with milk, and fitly set. His cheeks are as a bed of spices, as sweet flowers. His lips are like roses, dropping sweet-smelling myrrh. His hands are as gold rings set with the beryl. His body is as bright ivory overlaid with sapphires. His legs are as pillars of marble set upon sockets of fine gold. His countenance is as Lebanon, excellent as the cedars. His mouth is most sweet; yes, he is altogether lovely. This is my beloved, and this is my friend, O daughters of Jerusalem. Therefore you shall hold him <margin>Chapter 8:4</margin> and not let him go until you bring him into his mother’s house, into his mother’s chamber. 

And when Solomon had finished these words, I did not know how to answer him, but instead became speechless. Nevertheless, I intended to open the enclosed treasure again, so that I might find peace...

<summary>This page describes an alchemical dream allegory where the narrator receives a dirty "box" from a maiden, symbolizing the raw matter of the Great Work. Guided by an old woman and the wisdom of Solomon, he learns to purify this matter using "philosophical fire" and "lye" to reveal the inner treasure of silver (lunar) and gold (solar) qualities.</summary>

->Know then that the Lord leads his saints wonderfully. Psalm 4.<-

Since everything recorded in Holy Scripture was left to us for teaching, research, and remembrance—through which we humans are meant to rightly recognize our great and incomprehensible God, His noble handiwork in all creatures, and most of all, ourselves—and since the miracle-numbers Three, Four, and Seven are frequently mentioned in the Holy Bible, these numbers undoubtedly contain a great mystery hidden within them.

Therefore, I ask in simplicity and from a pure heart what these following things signify, both in the Light of Nature <note>The "Light of Nature" refers to knowledge gained through observing the physical world and alchemy, while the "Light of Grace" refers to divine revelation through scripture.</note> as well as in the Light of Grace.

# Of the Number Three.

First, what is the meaning of the three different days in Genesis 8, during which Noah released the raven and afterwards the dove from the ark, each time after twenty-one days <note>original: "dreymal sieben Tagen" (three times seven days).</note> had passed.

Second, what is the meaning of the very first sacrifice which the Lord God Himself commanded Abraham to perform, as read in Genesis 15:

->And the Lord God spoke to me:<-

And a turtle-dove, and a young pigeon, = = Eagles' embers. <note>original: "Gluten". This likely refers to the "heat" or "viscosity" associated with the volatile spirit in alchemy.</note>
And he brought it, and divided it in the middle = Philosophical Solution. <note>original: "Solutio Philosoph." - the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state.</note>
But the birds he did not divide, = = = = Sophist Separation. <note>In alchemy, a "sophist" process was considered a false or superficial chemical separation compared to the true "philosophical" work.</note>
And the birds of prey fell upon the carcasses, but he drove them away. <term>Caput Mortuum</term> <note>Literally "dead head"; the worthless residue or dregs left after a chemical distillation or sublimation.</note>

Hermes' Little Bird <note>A famous alchemical allegory for Mercury (quicksilver) or the volatile spirit that must be "fixed" or "killed" to become stable.</note> ☿ also devours the dead corpses and flies away with them; it is finally caught by the Philosopher, strangled, and killed.

Third, what kind of holy, miraculous fire was it, mentioned in Leviticus 9 and 2 Chronicles 7, which fell from heaven and ignited and consumed the offerings on the altar? This fire was later taken by the priests when they were led away into the Babylonian captivity; and as the Prophet Nehemiah (who is also called Jeremiah) commanded them to hide the holy fire in a pit until they should return home, they later had the priests search for it again. Instead of the fire, they found a thick water; but when this was poured upon the wood and the sacrifice, it was ignited by the sun, and the entire sacrifice together with the wood was burned and consumed by the fire and water. This is found in 2 Maccabees 1:19-20. Where is this same fire and water to be found and obtained today? It is the <term>Prima Materia</term> <note>The "First Matter" or primordial substance required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, or the <term>Spiritus Mundi</term> <note>The "World Spirit" or the universal animating force.</note>, in which gold is consumed, and after <term>Putrefaction</term> <note>The stage of rotting or decay in the alchemical process, seen as a necessary precursor to rebirth.</note> rises again to a new life.

Fourth, what is the meaning of the three great miracle-births in the Old and New Testaments, which occurred against and beyond the course of nature; which the Lord God Himself announced through His angels. First, that of Isaac (Genesis 15, 18, and 21). Then of Samson (Judges 13). Then of John the Baptist (Luke 1). And finally, the most wondrous birth of all: our Savior and Redeemer Jesus Christ, the Son of the Virgin, which far surpasses the other three births (Matthew and Luke 1).

Fifth, what do the three parts of man—1. the Spirit, 2. the Soul, 3. the Body—of which the Holy Apostle Paul writes in 1 Thessalonians 5, intend to signify and indicate, both in the Light of Nature and the Light of Grace.

->AND EVERYWHERE WELL THE ROOT OF DAVID <note>A Messianic title from the Book of Revelation referring to Jesus</note> HAS CONQUERED AND REIGNS FROM THE DAWN TO THE WEST WIND <note>original Latin: "ET UBIQUE BENE RADIX DAVIDIS VICIT REGNAT AB AURORA IN FAVONIO"</note><-

### JESUS

<meta>This page continues the introductory material of the Secret Figures of the Rosicrucians, transitioning from the 1788 title page to a specific alchemical treatise within the collection. ### A Golden Treatise on the <term>Philosopher’s Stone<note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life; in Rosicrucian thought, it also symbolized spiritual perfection.</note>.</term>
#### Written by a philosopher still living, yet unnamed, for the instruction of the <term>Sons of Doctrine<note>original: "Filiis Doctrinæ"; a traditional term for students or initiates of the alchemical arts.</note>,</term> but as a report for the <term>Brothers of the Golden Cross<note>original: "Fratribus aureæ Crucis"; an early designation for members of the Rosicrucian brotherhood.</note>.</term>

<image-desc>An emblem featuring an architectural frame with two columns. Inside a central circular border is the text "Work in hope for all things" <note>original: "LABE IN SPE OMNIA"</note> at the top and "Blessed Stone" <note>original: "LAPIS BENEDIC."</note> at the bottom. Within this circle is a rectangle with the text "All glory is an effort in the sand" <note>original: "OMNE DECUS NISUS IN ARENA"</note>. Below the emblem, the year is printed: "The year 1625" <note>original: "ANNO M. DC. XXV."</note></image-desc>

->**Preface**<-
->To the Reader Seeking the Art.<-

Do not be amazed, dear reader and righteous seeker of the secrets of Nature, why in this final age of the world—when it already stands with one foot in the grave <note>A common 17th-century sentiment that the Apocalypse or the end of the world was imminent.</note>—I have undertaken to write this short treatise. This is especially so since nearly all libraries are found full of books written on this subject, most of which, however, contain false and fabricated philosophy and recipes. I have written it not for my own sake, but for yours, to show you the foundation of truth and to lead you away from the wandering paths, for much depends on this for you. As for me, I already know what I need to know for this purpose; I have no need for books, having read as many of them in twenty-two years as I could get into my hands, of which there were not a few, both handwritten and printed. You will find herein the <term>matter<note>The raw material or "prima materia" used to start the alchemical work.</note></term> and the solution described theoretically, as well as the entire practice perfectly contained in an allegory, as clearly and plainly as you might seek from any philosopher. I have also endeavored to speak with the philosophers, and therefore I have precisely indicated all the places where they touch upon this or that point, so that you yourself may look them up and compare the concept and my citations <note>original: "allegata"</note> against one another, and thereby sharpen your understanding. Indeed, I could have described this treatise with less effort without such citations and revealed myself to the Brothers of the Golden Cross; but for you, as already mentioned, it is intended for the best, so that you may draw all the more understanding from it. You should also not wonder that I have hidden my name and have not wished to reveal myself to you personally: I do not seek my own vain glory, nor to make a great name for myself in the world, but rather your benefit. Moreover, my teachers—the true philosophers, I mean—did not teach me to risk my life for the sake of great prestige and to offer it up to spiritual robbers, nor to load great sins upon myself by prostituting <note>In this context, to "prostitute" means to make a sacred or secret thing common and vulgar by revealing it to the unworthy.</note> this great secret. The reader will have heard from <term>Sendivogius<note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and diplomat who claimed to have the Philosopher's Stone and often wrote under anagrams to protect his identity.</note></term> that as often as he revealed himself to great lords, it always resulted in harm and danger to him. Experience proves that various philosophers, who did not sufficiently guard their treasures, were strangled and robbed of their <term>tincture<note>The "tincture" is another name for the Philosopher's Stone, specifically its power to "dye" or transform metals.</note></term> by greedy and proud fellows who risked their souls for that purpose. Reason confirms it: everyone who carries a great treasure openly would not wish to be robbed. Sendivogius hid his name in his anagrams. A short time ago, a new Brother of the Golden Cross likewise made himself known with an anagram and an enigma, whose name is well known to me; why then should I place myself before the eyes of the whole world? Let it suffice you, dear friend, that I make myself known to the wise, and at the same time make my name known to my own, which I have done without fail; I commend the rest to our Lord God, who will make me known to you if it

The ancient and new philosophers, many kind readers, and followers of true wisdom—after they reached the goal of their desire through divine granting—were accustomed to make themselves known to others of their fellow associates, who remained hidden here and there in the world, through their writings. This was to indicate to them both that the faithful God had also enlightened their understanding, blessed the work of their hands, and revealed to them the great secret of this earthly wisdom, for which they are rightly bound to give Him praise, honor, and glory. And also, it was so that they might leave behind guidance and instruction for their fellow Christians and art-loving students, so that they too—if it should please the dear God—might attain knowledge and science of such secrets.
And such people have been among all kinds of nations, such as Egyptians, among whom <term>Hermes Trismegistus<note>The legendary "Thrice-Great" founder of alchemy and Hermetic philosophy.</note></term> was the most prominent; Chaldeans, Greeks, Arabs, Italians, French, English, Dutch, Spaniards, Germans, Poles, Hungarians, Jews, and others more. But it is not a little surprising that although the aforementioned wise men wrote in different languages and at different times, nevertheless such a harmony and unanimous consensus is found in their writings that every true philosopher can immediately recognize their intention, and that God has gladdened them with this great blessing and that they have the work itself in their hands. Likewise, because

<column-break/>

pleases Him and will be useful to me and to you. Do not let yourself be tempted to search for my name: for if you ever learned it or knew me, you would still have to be satisfied with this treatise. For I have sworn with <term>Bernard of Trevisan<note>original: "Bernhardo von Tresne"; a 15th-century Italian alchemist famous for his supposed discovery of the Stone after decades of failure.</note></term> and <term>Thomas Aquinas<note>likely the "Traugenten" mentioned, as Aquinas was often cited as an alchemical authority in this era.</note></term>, the philosophers, according to what is right, to reveal nothing further to anyone than what has happened herein. Do not worry either whether I have this treasure in my hands: ask much more whether I have seen how the world was created? How the darkness was in Egypt? What the cause of the rainbow is? How the clarified bodies will look after the general resurrection? Which color is the most permanent? But I ask you, who read my little book, whether you have seen the great general salty sea, without any corrosive, which is sufficient to carry the tinctures of all things to the highest mountains? Tell me, where does <term>sulphur<note>Sulphur and Mercury (Mercurio) are the two primary principles in alchemy representing the soul/fire and the spirit/water.</note></term> come from sulphur, and <term>mercury</term> from mercury? Also: where does sulphur come from mercury, and mercury from sulphur? When have your eyes beheld the image of fervent love, where man and woman embrace each other so that they can never again be separated in eternity, but for the sake of magnificent love become one thing? If you understand what I am now saying, and have worked such things with your hands and seen them with your eyes, then I am your companion and I notify you that I also know it, and intended to have your acquaintance, like that of another, through this teaching, for which purpose I also wanted to write this little treatise.
If there were anyone who complained about the difficulty of this art, let him know that it is not difficult in and of itself, and that for those who love God and are deemed worthy by Him, it comes quite easily.
But should someone accuse me of having written the art too brightly and clearly, so that everyone could understand it: to him I answer that I have indeed described it understandably enough for the worthy, to whom God grants such things, but the unworthy will likely leave it alone. I have previously told the entire art word for word to the over-clever; but they laughed at it in their hearts, not believing that there is a twofold resurrection of the dead in our work. Therefore, our art in theory and practice <note>original: "Theoria vnd Practica"</note> is a pure gift of God, which He gives when and to whom He wills, and it does not depend on anyone’s willing or running. I have known it with all circumstances and manual techniques for a good 17 or 18 years, and yet had to wait until it pleased God to grant me grace. No human being should doubt the certainty and truth of this art, since it is as true, as certain in nature, and as undoubtedly ordained by God as the sun shines by day and the moon lights the night. Herewith I will close this brief preface and begin the treatise itself. But you, beloved <term>Brothers of the Golden Cross<note>original: "fratres crucis aureæ"</note></term>, who keep yourselves hidden here and there in secret and enjoy the high gifts of God in His fear: do not withhold yourselves from me, and if you do not know me, then know that the Cross tests the faithful and brings their faith to light, but security and pleasure stifle it. God be with us all. AMEN.

truth emerges from harmony, so on the other hand, <term>dissonance<note>original: "Dissonantia"</note></term> must bring every <term>sophist<note>In alchemy, a "sophist" is a "false alchemist" or a "puffer" who uses deceptive methods or only seeks material wealth.</note></term> and pretended philosopher to light.
For because he has never rightly recognized the secret of the wise and goes his own way according to his own brain, every knower of the art sees his errors.
But the harmony and unanimous consensus consist primarily in these points, namely in the knowledge of the materials, their solution, weight, fire, and augmentation. Regarding the matter, it is a single one, which has within itself everything it needs, upon which everything that the lover of the art desires is made, namely "effort in the sand" <note>original: "nisus in arena"</note>, as the philosopher <term>Anaxagoras<note>original: "Anaxtratus"; likely referring to the Greek philosopher Anaxagoras, who appears as a character in the *Turba Philosophorum*.</note></term> says in the <term>*Turba*<note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers) is one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a meeting of ancient philosophers.</note>:</term> There is nothing more precious than the red sand of the sea, and it is the spittle of the moon, which is added to the light of the sun and coagulated.
But that such a single matter is required, <term>Agadmon<note>Another philosopher character from the *Turba Philosophorum*.</note></term> testifies in that same place, saying: know that if you do not take this body of mine, which has no spirit, you will not attain what you seek; and that is because nothing foreign enters the work, nor is anything else required for it other than what is pure. Therefore, let all multiplicity go: for Nature is satisfied with only a single thing, and whoever does not know that will perish. <term>Arnold of Villanova<note>original: "Arnoldus de Villa noua"; a major 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note></term> carries the same saying in his little book *The Flower* <note>original: "Flos"</note>.

<summary>This preface introduces "A Golden Treatise on the Philosopher’s Stone," emphasizing that true alchemical wisdom is a divine gift and that the author remains anonymous to avoid the dangers and vanity of worldly fame. It asserts that despite different languages and eras, true alchemists share a unanimous consensus on the "single matter" required for the work.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Rosicrucianism, Michael Sendivogius, Turba Philosophorum, alchemy, spiritual secrecy, divine grace, Anaxagoras, Arnold of Villanova</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Golden Treatise on the Philosopher’s Stone." It includes a title page with an emblematic image and a preface addressed to the "Sons of Doctrine" and the "Brothers of the Golden Cross," establishing the author's intent to provide a clear, allegorical guide to alchemy during what he considers the end-times of the world.</meta>

# A Golden Treatise on the Philosopher’s Stone.
## Written by a philosopher still living, yet unnamed, for the instruction of the Sons of Doctrine <note>original: "Filiis Doctrinæ"</note>, and for the notification of the Brothers of the Golden Cross <note>original: "Fratribus aureæ Crucis"</note>.

<image-desc>An ornamental circular diagram or vessel design. Inside the top part are the words "All Glory" <note>original: "OMNE DECUS"</note>. Running vertically down the center is the phrase "The Blessed Stone contains all things within itself" <note>original: "HABET IN SE OMNIA LAPIS BENEDICT."</note>. At the bottom are the words "Effort in the sand" <note>original: "NISUS IN ARENA"</note>. The entire design is set within an architectural frame.</image-desc>

-> IN THE YEAR 1625 <-

---

-> Preface <-
-> To the Reader Seeking the Art. <-

Do not wonder, dear reader and righteous investigator of nature’s secrets, why in this final age of the world—when she already walks with one foot in the grave—I have undertaken to write this short treatise. This is especially so since nearly all libraries are found full of books written on this matter, the majority of which nevertheless contain false and fabricated philosophy and recipes. I have written it not for my sake, but for yours, to show you the foundation of truth and to lead you away from the erring paths, which is of no small importance to you. As for me, I already know what is necessary for me to know in this regard; I need no books, having read as many of them in twenty-two years as I could get into my hands, of which there were not a few, both written and printed. You will find herein the matter and the solution described theoretically, as well as the entire practice perfectly contained in an allegory, as clearly and plainly as you might seek from any philosopher. I have also endeavored to speak with the philosophers, and therefore I have properly indicated all places where they use this or that saying, so that you may look them up yourself and compare the concepts and my citations <note>original: "allegata"</note> against one another, and thereby sharpen your understanding. Indeed, I could have written this treatise with less effort without such citations and made myself known to the Brothers of the Golden Cross; but for you, as I said, it is intended for the best, so that you may draw all the more understanding from it. You should also not wonder that I have hidden my name and do not wish to reveal myself to you personally: I do not seek my own vain glory or to make a great name for myself before the world, but rather your benefit. Moreover, my teachers—I mean the true philosophers—have not taught me to risk my life for the sake of great prestige and to offer myself for sale to greedy robbers, nor to load great sins upon myself by prostituting this great secret. The reader will have heard from Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and diplomat</note> that as often as he revealed himself to great lords, it always resulted in harm and danger for him. Experience testifies that various philosophers who did not sufficiently guard their treasures were strangled and robbed of their Tincture <note>The "Tincture" or "Powder of Projection" is the finished Alchemical Stone used to transmute metals</note> by greedy and proud fellows who risked their souls for that purpose. Reason confirms that anyone carrying a great treasure openly would not wish to be robbed. Sendivogius hid his name in his anagrams. A short time ago, a new Brother of the Golden Cross likewise made himself known with an anagram and an enigma, whose name is well known to me. Why then should I place myself before the eyes of the whole world? Let it suffice you, dear friend, that I make myself known to the wise, and at the same time make my name known to you, which I have done without fail; commit the rest to our Lord God, who will make me known to you if it 

The ancient and new philosophers, much-beloved reader and follower of true wisdom, after they reached the goal of their desire through Divine grant, were accustomed to make themselves known to others of their fellows who remained hidden here and there in the world through their writings. This was to indicate to them, first, that the faithful God had also enlightened their understanding, blessed the work of their hands, and revealed to them the great secret of this earthly wisdom, for which they are rightly bound to give Him collective praise, honor, and glory. Secondly, it was so that they might leave behind guidance and news for their fellow Christians and art-seeking students, so that they too—if it should so please the dear God—might attain the knowledge and science of such secrets.

And such people have been among all kinds of nations, such as Egyptians (among whom Hermes Trismegistus <note>The legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy</note> was the most prominent), Chaldeans, Greeks, Arabs, Italians, French, English, Netherlanders, Spaniards, Germans, Poles, Hungarians, Jews, and others more. However, it is no small wonder that although the aforementioned wise men wrote in different languages and at different times, such an agreement and unanimous consensus is nevertheless found in their writings that every true philosopher can immediately recognize that God has gladdened them with this great blessing and that they have the work itself in their hands; likewise, because 

<column-break/>

pleases Him, and will be useful to me and to you. Do not let yourself lust to search for my name: for even if you ever learned it or knew me, you would still have to be satisfied with this treatise. For I have sworn, along with Bernard of Treviso <note>A 15th-century Italian alchemist</note> and Rhazes <note>An influential 9th-century Persian polymath and alchemist</note> the philosophers, as is right, to reveal nothing further to anyone than what has been done herein. Do not trouble yourself either as to whether I have this treasure in my hands: ask much more whether I have seen how the world was created? How the darkness was in Egypt? What the cause of the rainbow is? How the glorified bodies <note>Refers to the perfected state of matter, or "clarificirten Leiber"</note> will look after the general resurrection? Which is the most permanent color? But I ask you, who understand my little book, whether you have seen the universal great salty sea, without any corrosive, which is sufficient to carry the tinctures of all things to the highest mountains? Tell me, where does sulphur become sulphur, and mercury become mercury? Also: where does mercury become sulphur, and sulphur become mercury? Have your eyes beheld the image of fervent love, where man and woman embrace so that they can never be separated again in eternity, but instead, out of heartfelt love, become one thing? If you understand what I am now saying, and have worked such with your hands and seen it with your eyes, then I am your companion, and I notify you that I also know it, and nothing would be dearer to me than to have your secret acquaintance, for which intent I have also wished to write this little treatise.

Should there be someone who complains about the difficulty of this art, let him know that it is not difficult in and of itself, and that for those who love God and are deemed worthy by Him for this, it comes quite easily.

But should someone wish to accuse me of having written the art too brightly and clearly, so that everyone can understand it: to him I answer that I have indeed described it understandably enough for the worthy, to whom God grants such things; the unworthy, however, will likely leave it alone. I have previously told the entire art to the over-clever from word to word; they, however, laughed at it in their hearts, not believing that there is a twofold resurrection of the dead in our work. Therefore, our art in theory and practice is a pure gift of God, which He gives when and to whom He wills, and it depends not on any man’s will or effort. I have known it with all circumstances and maneuvers for a good 17 or 18 years, and yet I had to wait until it pleased God to grant me grace. No human being should doubt the certainty and truth of this art, since it is as true, as certain in nature, and as undoubtedly ordained by God as the sun shines by day and the moon lights the night. Herewith I wish to close this brief preface and begin the treatise itself. But you, beloved Brothers of the Golden Cross, who keep yourselves hidden here and there in secret and enjoy the high gifts of God in His fear, do not withhold yourselves from me; and if you do not know me, then know that the cross proves the faithful and brings their faith to light, but security and pleasure stifle it. God be with us all. AMEN.

truth emerges from harmony, so conversely, dissonance must bring every sophist and presumed philosopher to light.

For because he never rightly recognized the secret of the wise and follows his own path according to his own brain, every knower of the art sees his error.

The harmony and agreement consist primarily in these points: namely, in the knowledge of the materials, their solution, weight, fire, and augmentation. Regarding the matter <note>original: "Materiam"</note>, it is a single one which has within itself everything it needs, from which everything that the art-lover desires is made, namely effort in the sand <note>original: "nisus in arena"</note>, as the philosopher Anaxagoras says in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>: <gloss>There is nothing more precious than the red sand of the sea, and it is the spittle of the moon, which is added to the light of the sun and coagulated.</gloss>

But that such a single matter is required is testified by Agadmon in the same place, saying: know that if you do not take this, my body, which has no spirit, you will not attain what you seek; and that is because nothing foreign enters the work, nor is anything else required for it except that which is pure. Therefore, let all multiplicity go: for nature is satisfied with only one single thing, and whoever does not know that will perish. A similar saying is used by Arnold of Villanova <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note> in his little book *The Flower* <note>original: "Flos"</note>

<summary>This page serves as the introduction to an alchemical treatise, where the anonymous author defends the clarity of his writing while insisting that the "Art" is a divine gift. He establishes the core alchemical principle that the Great Work requires only one primary, pure material.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Golden Treatise," moving from the preface into the core philosophical arguments regarding the "One Thing"—the single source material of the Philosopher's Stone. It reconciles the apparent contradiction of authors who describe the Stone as being made of two things (like the "Rebis") by explaining their underlying unity.</meta>

called: our Stone is made from one thing, and with one thing. Likewise, he says to the King of Naples: Everything that is in our Stone is necessary to it, and it needs nothing else, since the Stone is of one nature and is one thing. And <term>Rosinus</term> <note>A name often associated with Zosimos of Panopolis in Latin alchemy</note> says: Be assured that there is only one thing from which everything you desire is made. And <term>Lilium</term>: You need no more than one thing, which in every stage of our Work transforms into another nature. Thus also speaks <term>Geber</term> <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the famous 8th-century polymath</note> in his *Summa*: It is one Stone, one medicine, to which we add nothing, nor take anything away, but only separate the superfluous from it. And <term>Scites</term> in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* or "Assembly of the Philosophers," an early Latin alchemical text</note> says: the foundation of this art is something unique, which is stronger and higher than all things, and is called the sharp vinegar, which has caused the Gold to become a pure spirit, without which neither the Whiteness, nor the Blackness, nor the Redness can exist: and when it is mixed with the body, it is retained with it, and becomes one with it, and transforms it into a spirit, and colors it with a spiritual and unchangeable color, and receives from the colored one in return its bodily color, which cannot be extinguished: and if you were to set the body to the fire without vinegar, it would burn.

However, someone might conclude from these words of <term>Scites</term> that not one, but two things, namely the Body and the Vinegar (as he calls it), are required, and that one must necessarily join a Wet and a Dry thing together so that the dry is not burned by the fire but protected from such burning by the wet. To such an argument and conclusion I must rightly agree—if it is understood correctly—and nonetheless maintain the dignity and truth of the established philosophical sayings. For it is certain that there is only one single material of our blessed Stone, which has very many names among the Sages, which Nature prepares for the artist, and which is ordained as the material of the Great Stone alone and exclusively, and otherwise no other thing in the world.

This material is before everyone's eyes; the whole world sees it, grasps it, loves it, and yet does not know it: It is noble and common, dear and cheap, precious and lowly, and is found in all places. <term>Theophrastus Paracelsus</term> calls it in his book *Tinctura Physicorum* the Red Lion, named by many, known by few. <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus</note> calls it in the first chapter of his book Mercury, which is hardened in the innermost chambers. In the *Turba* it is called here and there Bronze or Ore: in the *Rosarium Philosophorum* <note>The "Rose Garden of the Philosophers," a 16th-century treatise</note> it is called Salt. In short, this material has as many names as there are things in the world. From this it also happens that it is so little understood by the ignorant. I call them ignorant because they proceed to the Art without prior knowledge of Nature and her properties, like a goat going to the manger, not knowing what it is stretching its mouth for, as <term>Arnoldus</term> <note>Arnold of Villanova</note> says.

Therefore, <term>Geber</term> says well and rightly in his *Summa perfectionis* <note>original: "The Sum of Perfection"</note>: He who does not know the beginnings of Nature within himself is still far removed from this Art. And <term>Rosarius</term> says: I advise that no one let themselves in to find this Art unless they recognize the beginning of true Nature and its order: When he has recognized these, then he needs no more than a single thing, and it does not require great expense: For it is no more than one Stone, one medicine, one vessel, one order, and one arrangement. Yet our material is thus divided through the help of Nature and the clever handiwork of the artist, so that, as <term>Theophrastus</term> says, it is transformed into the White Eagle, so that the brilliance of the Sun <note>original: "Solis"</note> does not outshine the alchemist <note>original: "Spagyro"</note>, or (as <term>Basilius Valentinus</term> calls it) from it becomes a Spirit white as snow, and yet another Spirit red as blood, which both Spirits have the third hidden within them. Therefore King <term>Aros</term> speaks not poorly when he says: Our medicine is made of two of one essence, namely from the union of the constant, spiritual and bodily, cold and moist, warm and dry nature, and can be made from no other thing. And <term>Richardus Anglicus</term> <note>Richard the Englishman</note> says: It is one Stone, and one medicine, which is called by the Philosophers <term>Rebis</term> <note>From the Latin *res bis*, meaning "a double thing"</note>, that is, out of two things, namely from the body and the spirit, White or Red: in which many fools have erred in different ways, interpreting the verse:

> In the figures mentioned, the Rebis is the most correct rule. <note>original: "Est rebis in dictis rectissima norma figuris."</note>

That is, two things: and these two things are one thing, namely the Water that is added to the body, and dissolves it into a spirit; that is, into a mineral water from which it was originally made, and thus from the body and spirit comes a mineral water, which is called <term>Elixir</term>, that is, a <term>Fermentum</term> <note>original: "ferment"</note>. For then the water and the spirit are one thing, from which the tincture and medicine are made, which purify all bodies. Therefore our medicine is perfected from one thing, which is the water and spirit of the body. And thus we have, according to the Philosophers, drawn the nature of Sulfur and Mercury above the earth, clean and pure, etc. And <term>Basilius Valentinus</term> says in the 4th chapter of his book *Of Natural and Supernatural Things*: I will reveal this to you in truth and through the love of God, that the root of Philosophical Sulfur, which is a Heavenly Spirit, is one with the root of the spiritual supernatural <term>Mercury</term>, as well as the beginning of the spiritual Salt; they are in one, and are found in one material, from which the Stone (which was before us) is made, and not in many things. Although Mercury for itself, and Sulfur for itself, and Salt in particular are treated by all Philosophers—so that Mercury is found in one, Sulfur in one, and Salt in one—I tell you nevertheless that this is only to be understood of their abundance, which is found most in each, and in many ways can be used and prepared with profit for medicine and the transformation of metals: But the <term>Universal</term> <note>The "Universal" medicine or Philosopher's Stone</note> as the highest treasure of earthly wisdom and the beginning of all three things is a single thing, and is found and drawn out of a single thing at the same time, which can make all metals into one; it is the true <term>Spirit of Mercury</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Mercurij"</note> and <term>Soul of Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Anima Sulphuris"</note> together with the spiritual Salt, united and enclosed under one heaven, and dwelling in one body, and it is the Dragon and the Eagle, it is the King and the Lion, it is the Spirit and the Corpse, which must color the corpse of Gold into a medicine, etc. Thus our prepared material is called the Man and the Woman.

Also the active and the passive, as <term>Zimon</term> says in the *Turba*: know that the secret of this work consists of the Man and the Woman, that is, of the active and the passive. In Lead is the Man, in Orpiment <note>Arsenic trisulfide, a yellow mineral</note> is the Woman. The Man rejoices in his accepted Woman, and is helped by her: and the Woman receives from the Man a coloring seed, and is colored by him. And <term>Diamedes</term> speaks: Join the masculine son of the red servant to his sweet-smelling wife, and they, being thus joined together, will give birth to the Art, into which you shall introduce nothing foreign, no powder, nor any other thing, and let the conception suffice for you, so the right son will be born to you: Oh, how exceedingly precious is the material of this red servant, without which no order can exist: Others call it <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argentum viuum"</note> or <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Sulfur</term> or Fire. As <term>Rogerius Baco</term> <note>Roger Bacon</note> says in the *Speculum* <note>original: "Mirror of Alchemy"</note> chapter 3: From Sulfur and Mercury

<column-break/>

all metals are born, and nothing clings to them, nor is anything added to them, nor does anything change them, except what comes from them. Therefore we must also rightly take <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Sulfur</term> for the <term>Material</term> <note>original: "Materiam"</note> of the Stone. And <term>Menabadus</term>: He who joins the Quicksilver to the body of the <term>Magnesia</term>, and the Woman to the Man, draws out the hidden nature with which the bodies are colored.

And <term>Lullius</term> <note>Ramon Llull</note> in his *Codicillo*: The property of our <term>Mercury</term> is that it allows itself to be <term>Coagulated</term> <note>thickened or solidified</note> by its Sulfur. And in the *Practica* of his Testament he says: Quicksilver is an overflowing and flowing moisture that is preserved from burning. Others call it the Body, Spirit, and Soul. Thus speaks <term>Arnoldus in flore florum</term> <note>original: "The Flower of Flowers"</note>: The <term>Philosophers</term> have said our Stone is composed of the Body, the Soul, and the Spirit, and they have spoken the truth: For they compared the imperfect body <note>original: "Corpus"</note> to the Body because it is weak; the Water they called the Spirit, and that with truth, because it is a spirit; but the <term>Ferment</term> <note>original: "Fermentum"</note> they expressed with the name Soul, because it gives life to the imperfect body, which it did not have before, and brings forth a better form. And a little before he says: the spirit is not added to its body except through the mediation of the soul: For the soul is the middle between the body and the spirit, which joins the two together. And <term>Morienus</term> speaks: The soul enters quickly into its body; but if you wanted to add it to another body, you would labor in vain. And <term>Lilium</term>: The Soul, the Body, and the Spirit are together, and it is one thing which has everything within itself, and to which nothing foreign is added. But why is it necessary to cite and explain all the names by which this material of ours is called? We shall be satisfied with these as the most common and closest to our purpose. And after we have actually investigated where this material of ours must come from and be obtained, we want to consider the Solution, as the main part of the whole Art, for a little while, and sharpen our understanding through that same consideration.

Concerning now the <term>Consideration</term> and weighing of what our material is, and where one must get it: it is to be known that the Almighty Creator, whose wisdom is as great as He Himself, namely infinite, created two kinds of things in the beginning when nothing but He Himself existed: namely the Heavenly, and those that are under the Heaven. The Heavenly are the Heavens themselves and the Heavenly inhabitants, about which we do not wish to philosophize extensively at this time. The works created under the Heaven are those created from the four elements, and in their number only three kinds of lineages are found: namely, first, everything that has life and feeling, and are called <term>Animals</term> <note>original: "Animalia"</note>; after that, everything that grows out of the earth and does not feel, and are called <term>Vegetables</term> <note>original: "Vegetabilia"</note>. Finally, everything that grows under the earth, and are called <term>Minerals</term> <note>original: "Mineralia"</note>.

These three lineages of creatures now encompass everything that was created under the moon from the four elements, and there are found neither more nor less of them; they are also so fixed by the Highest God, each in its lineage and its kind, that none can be transformed from its kind and lineage into another. As if one wanted to make a human or a tree out of a stone; or an ape or lead out of a herb; or another animal or herb out of lead. Such a thing, I say, is impossible by the decree of the Great King. For if such were permitted in Nature, there could be fewer lineages, or indeed all could be transformed into one. But because everything would then fall into a heap, the Lord of all hearts did not want to permit such a change of lineages: And what is more, He not only maintained these three lineages each in its kind, but also wanted to incorporate into each lineage its own seed, so that each might thereby be multiplied and remain in its form, and not this form be changed into another's form—as a human into a horse, or an apple tree into lettuce, or a diamond or other stone into gold. Such a thing, I say, is not permitted in the nature of our things. And as it has been from the beginning, so it shall remain until the day when the Almighty, who said in the beginning "Let it be," shall say "Let it pass away." It is, however, quite permitted that among those things which have a common <term>Material</term> <note>original: "Materiam"</note>, seed, and composition of elements, an ennobling and improvement of its state can be performed and perfected, according to whether their material is pure and perfect.

As one sees that a human of much higher and more understanding mind rises much higher than others who do not have such sharp and subtle understanding—which then arises and springs from the pure and subtle spirits, from the justified and well-tempered constitution of the body. Thus one also sees how one horse is much nobler than another, and so nearly in all <term>Species of animals</term> <note>original: "Speciebus animalium"</note>. Now, as this holds true in the animal lineages, so it is also found abundantly in the herbs and trees. In trees through planting, grafting, and other means known to diligent gardeners; in herbs one sees daily how herbs and flowers of one kind are each more noble, beautiful, sweet-smelling, better, and tastier than the others: one only need look at the <term>Carnations</term> <note>original: "Garoyphyllos"</note> or cloves and the tulips—I will not speak of others at this time—how many kinds there are of them, so that one can hardly count them, which, through diligent care and improvement, become nobler and nobler, so that such beautiful and sweet-smelling flowers come forth, the likes of which, it seems, have never grown before.

What shall I now say of the metals, whose general material is Quicksilver, which is cooked and coagulated by Sulfur? As <term>Richardus Anglicus</term> says in chapter 6: Nature has worked the lineage of all fluid and meltable things upon the essence of Quicksilver and its Sulfur: for the property of Quicksilver is that it allows itself to be coagulated by the vapor, as if by the heat of the Sulfur, which is Red or White and does not burn. And <term>Arnoldus</term> says in his first part, chapter 2: Of the perfection of the Mastery: Quicksilver is the beginning of all things that can be melted, since all meltable things, when they are melted, are transformed into the same, and it allows itself to be mixed with them because it is of their essence: although those same bodies are different from Quicksilver in their composition, in such a manner, according to whether it was pure or impure, from the impure Sulfur, which is foreign and contrary to it. And <term>Rosinus</term> says to <term>Saratanta</term>: The material of all metals is the cooked and imperfect Quicksilver, which the Sulfur cooks in the belly of the earth: and according to how the Sulfur is different, so are different metals born in the earth, because they all have one and the same general beginning material, only that more or less action comes to it, or does not come to it, with moderation.

Therefore we daily see before our eyes how Nature herself strives in constant labor to purify them and bring them to more perfection, and to make them into Gold, which is Nature's final intention, as we then see in all metals that Nature has begun to work in them: since none is found so poor that it does not carry a grain of silver or gold with it, and indeed it is so with the metals that Nature immediately wants to and can make Gold out of the Quicksilver that has its own Sulfur with it,

<meta>catchword: Quicksilver,</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical principle that the Philosopher's Stone is made from a single, universal material, often described through metaphors like the "Rebis" or the union of Mercury and Sulfur. It explains that while species are immutable, metals can be ennobled and perfected into gold because they share a common elemental root.</summary>
<keywords>Materia Prima, Rebis, Mercury, Sulfur, Quicksilver, Alchemy, perfection of metals, three kingdoms, Basilius Valentinus, Arnold of Villanova, Geber</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "One Thing," specifically identifying it within the mineral kingdom. It moves from general principles to a critique of misguided alchemists who seek the Stone in organic matter, grounding the argument in the classical "Mercury-Sulfur" theory of metallic composition.</meta>

...if no foreign matter intervenes and the impure, stinking, and combustible <term>Sulfur</term> <note>Sulfur and Mercury were seen as the two primary components of all metals; Sulfur provided the "form" and "combustibility," while Mercury provided the "matter" and "fluidity."</note> does not prevent it—as we see in many places where pure and fine Gold is found without the admixture of other metals.

However, because a foreign sulfur usually enters the mercury in the earth's veins, contaminating it and hindering its perfection, different metals are born according to the nature of this foreign sulfur. As <term>Aristotle</term> says in his *Meteorologica*, Book 4:

> If the essence of the <term>Mercury</term> is good but the combustible sulfur is impure, it transforms it into Copper. But if the mercury is stony, impure, and earthy, and the sulfur is also impure, Iron is formed from it. It seems, however, that Tin has good, pure mercury but a bad and poorly mixed sulfur. Lead, meanwhile, has a coarse, bad, heavy, and clay-like mercury, and a bad, tasteless, and stinking sulfur, which is why it does not easily <term>coagulate</term> <note>Coagulation is the process of a liquid substance turning solid, a key step in the "maturation" of metals in the earth.</note>.

This obstructive, combustible, and stinking sulfur is not the true "fire" that thoroughly cooks the metals; rather, Mercury has its own internal sulfur which performs this task. As <term>Bernard, Count of Trevisan</term> says: Many vainly believe that a sulfurous material is added during the generation of metals; but on the contrary, it is obvious that when Nature works, Mercury’s own sulfur is enclosed within it. This internal sulfur only rules through warm movement, by which the said sulfur and the other two properties of Mercury are changed. In this way, the various metals are born in the different parts of the earth. For in metals, as <term>Arnold [of Villanova]</term> says in the 1st chapter of his *Rosary*, there is a twofold superfluity: one is locked within the innermost essence of the Mercury itself, having mixed with it at the beginning of its being; the other comes from outside its nature and corrupts it. This latter one can be separated with great effort, but the former cannot be removed by any artist’s ingenuity.

Therefore, the great heat of the fire separates the combustible moisture from the metals, because the Mercury retains and protects that which is of its own nature from burning, while it rejects and allows the foreign matter to burn. The internal sulfur, which "cooks" the Mercury and brings it to maturity, is equally pure or impure, combustible or incombustible. The combustible sulfur prevents the Mercury from reaching perfection, so it cannot become Gold until that sulfur is finally separated from it. When only the pure, incombustible, and constant sulfur remains, then it becomes Gold or Silver, depending on whether that sulfur is white or red. This internal sulfur is nothing other than a "mature" Mercury, or the most ripened and ready part of the Mercury; that is why the Mercury accepts it so gladly but leaves other foreign sulfurs aside. As <term>Richardus [Anglicus]</term> says in chapter 9: The simpler and purer the sulfur is, the more it rejoices in simple and pure Mercury, and it clings to it so that one is more strongly joined with the other, and thus more perfect metals are born from them.

Such sulfur is not found upon the earth, as <term>Avicenna</term> says, except for what is found in the two bodies—namely, Gold and Silver—and indeed, it is much more mature in Gold. As Richardus says in chapter 12: The red sulfur is in Gold through greater maturation, and the white sulfur is in Silver through lesser maturation.

If all this is so—that there is a single and universal material for metals which becomes Gold (the end-goal of metals and the perfect intention of Nature) either quickly through its innate sulfur or over a long time through constant "cooking" in the earth—then we must confess that Nature seeks its own improvement and perfection within its own nature, according to the purity and subtlety of the subject, just as it does in the vegetable and animal kingdoms.

I have wished to point this out in a somewhat more detailed way, dear seeker of natural things, so that you may more fundamentally understand from where you must obtain the material of our Great Stone. For if you should undertake to make our Stone from animal matter, you will be blocked because the two belong to different "families": the Stone is mineral, but the material is animal. And from no thing, as Richardus says in chapter 1, can something be extracted that is not already within it. Therefore, because every species seeks to increase its power within its own species, and every genus within its own genus, and every nature brings fruit according to its own natural properties—and not in another nature that is contrary to it—it follows that every seed must agree with its own kind. And <term>Basil Valentine</term> speaks:

> Take heed and understand, my friend, that you should not seek to choose an animal soul for this. Your flesh and blood, as granted and given by the Creator to animals, belongs to the animals; from it, God built them together so that they became a beast.

Therefore, it is to be wondered at those who consider themselves great artists and yet seek their "Material of the Stone" in <note>original: "Menstruis muliebribus"</note> menstrual blood, in sperm, in eggs, in hair, in urine, and other such things, filling so many books with such recipes, and misleading and deceiving the less-informed with such worthless things.

<term>Roger Bacon</term>, in chapter 3 of the *Mirror [of Alchemy]*, wonders at the folly of such people, saying:

> It is to be wondered that a prudent man would base his intention on animal and vegetable things, which are so far removed, when minerals are found which are much closer.

It is by no means to be believed that any Philosopher placed the Art in the aforementioned distant things, unless it was by way of an allegory. Also, our Stone, as Basil Valentine says, does not arise from things that are combustible. For our Stone and its material are safe from all danger of fire. Therefore, you may well cease searching in animal things, as Nature has not allowed it to be found in them. If someone wished to seek our Stone in vegetable things, such as in trees, herbs, or flowers, they would err no less than one who wanted to make a great Stone out of an animal. For all herbs and trees, along with everything that comes from them, are combustible; nothing remains behind but a mere salt and its earth, which it received from Nature in its composition. And let no one be led astray by those who claim to make the Philosopher’s Stone from wheat; much less listen to those who undertake to make this Stone from wine and such things. Because they do not rightly understand the writings of <term>Raymond Lull</term>, they prove with their "great understanding" that they understand nothing, deceiving both themselves and others. It is true that very glorious and excellent <term>menstrua</term> <note>In alchemy, a "menstruum" is a solvent or a liquid used to extract the essence of a substance.</note> can be prepared from these, without which nothing noble can be accomplished in either medicine or alchemy;

<column-break/>

but that the Philosopher’s Stone could be made from them, or that its seed could be drawn from them, has not been permitted to Nature by the Creator of all things; rather, as mentioned above, they remain fixed in their own kind.

Therefore, every sensible person must easily perceive and conclude that, because our Stone is incombustible (as stated) and animal and vegetable things are all combustible, the Stone must be sought and made from an incombustible material, which is found nowhere else but in the Mineral kingdom.

Since our said Philosopher’s Stone is a Mineral work, one rightly asks—because there are many kinds, such as stones (among which various earths are understood), salts, "middle minerals," and metals—from which of these must it finally be made?

To this I answer: it is not possible to make it from stones, because there is no fluid or meltable Mercury in them; they cannot be melted, dissolved, or brought back to their "First Matter" because of their coarse sulfur and the superfluity of their earthy properties. Likewise, no wise seeker of natural secrets will seek the material of the Philosopher’s Stone in salts, alums, and similar things. For in these he will find nothing but a sharp, corrosive, and destructive spirit, but no Mercury or Sulfur at all, as the Philosophers require.

No "middle mineral"—such as <term>Magnesia</term>, <term>Marcasite</term>, <term>Antimony</term>, etc.—can become a metal from such things. How then could the material of the Philosopher’s Stone be taken from them, when it is the end and perfection of all metals and mineral things? Furthermore, these have no community or friendship with metals; instead, they burn, break, and ruin them. How then can they serve for their improvement? Listen to what <term>Richard the Englishman</term> says of this in chapter 10: The lesser minerals cannot become metals, firstly because they are not born from the First Matter of metals, which is Mercury.

Because their generation is different from the generation of Mercury from the start—in form, material, and composition—no metals can come from them, since there is only one First Matter and seed for a single thing from which it is born. This seems clear because the lesser minerals are not born from Mercury, as is evident from Aristotle and Avicenna. Therefore, if metals were to be made from them, they would first have to be brought back into the First Matter.

But because this cannot happen through any art, they can neither be metals nor, ultimately, the material of the Stone. Furthermore, since the lesser minerals cannot be the "beginning" of the Art (which is Mercury), they cannot reach the "middle" and the "end" (which are the metals and the <term>Tincture</term>). Instead, because the lesser minerals are foreign to the nature of metals—even if they share some mineral power—they are of weaker virtue and are combustible. Therefore, the metallic nature does not rejoice in them at all, but rejects them and keeps only what is of its own nature. Thus, those are fools who bring to light so many varied works and deceits to trick people—namely, using unpurified things that neither possess the Nature nor can give it.

Let no one be led astray by the writings of the Philosophers when they occasionally speak of "Salts," such as when it is said in the *Allegories of the Wise*: "He who works without salt will not raise the dead corpses." And in the book *Soliloquium* it says: "He who works without salt shoots with a bow without a string." For they have a very different meaning than that of mineral salts. This is clearly seen in the *Rosarium Philosophorum* when it says: "The Salt of the metals is the Philosopher’s Stone." For our Stone is a "coagulated water" in Gold or Silver, and it resists the fire and is dissolved in its water, from which it was made in its kind. That the "coagulated water" of the Philosophers is not mineral water, but their Mercury, is taught by <term>Geber</term> in the *Book of the Furnace*, chapter 19, where he says: "Prepare yourself to extract the dry water from the Sun and Moon, which the common man calls Mercury." The Philosophers also sometimes call their speeches "Salt," as seen in the *Clangore Buccinae* <note>original: "The Sound of the Trumpet"</note>, where it is said: "Note that the bodies are alum and salt, which flow out of our bodies." Sometimes they even call the Medicine itself "Salt," as it says in the *Scala*: "The work of the second water is that it heats the earth into its wonderful salt, solely through its attractive power." And Arnold says in the *Book on the Preservation of Youth*: "But that which has no equal in preserving youth is the salt from the <term>minera</term>." The wise have compared this, when it is prepared, to the natural warmth of a healthy youth; because of this analogy, they named the Stone with the names of animals, others called it "Mineral Chifir," and some called it an everlasting medicine and <note>original: "Aquam Vitæ"</note> the Water of Life. The entire science of its preparation is that it be reduced into a pure and drinkable water, with those things that have the same property as it.

From this it is now clearly seen that neither according to the teaching of the Philosophers, nor the property of Nature itself, can the material of the Stone be taken from the lesser minerals.

Now let us look around a bit to see if our Great Stone's material can be made from the "middle minerals," such as <term>Marcasite</term>, <term>Antimony</term>, <term>Magnesia</term>, and others, especially since the Philosophers mention them so often. As when <term>Senior</term> says: "If the power to coagulate Mercury were not in Orpiment, our mastery would never come to an end." And <term>Thomas Aquinas</term>: "Take our Antimony or the hooked black earth, etc." And <term>Parmenides</term> in the *Turba*: "Take mercury and coagulate it in the body of Magnesia, or in the sulfur that does not burn."

Yet it is important to know that the Philosophers do not speak this way because they mean that our Great Stone can be made from such things; rather, they speak only by way of analogy. For the "Orpiment" and "Magnesia" of the Philosophers are very different things than the common ones; they are the material itself, which they otherwise call the Agent, the Lion, the King, the Sulfur, and by many more names. It is called Orpiment because it has the power to turn Gold into an abundant color, and it is called Magnesia because of its great virtue and glory that springs and flows from it.

As for Thomas Aquinas calling it Antimony, he does so because of the black shimmering color it takes on after dissolution. For when our Stone has become black, the Philosophers compared it to all black things.

But someone might argue and say to me that some of these middle minerals are not only born from Mercury and Sulfur but also become metals—as one sees that Magnesia or Bismuth mixes with Lead and Tin in flux.

the...

<summary>This page argues that the Philosopher's Stone must be derived from a mineral source because organic materials are combustible and lack the necessary "metallic" spirit. It defines the true "Salt" and "Sulfur" of the alchemists as internal, non-combustible principles found only within the metallic kingdom, specifically within Mercury, Gold, and Silver.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Sulfur, Aristotle, Basil Valentine, Alchemy, Mineral Kingdom, Philosopher's Stone, Orpiment, Antimony, Magnesia, Coagulation, Transmutation, Metallic Generation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the "One Thing" (the prima materia) of the Philosopher's Stone. It moves from citing authorities on the unity of the material to a broader philosophical discussion on the three kingdoms of nature—Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral—and the possibility of "ennobling" or perfecting substances within their own species, specifically focusing on how Nature's ultimate goal is to turn all metals into gold.</meta>

...called them: our Stone is made from one thing, and with one thing. Likewise, he says to the King of Naples: Everything that is in our Stone is necessary to it, and it needs nothing else, since the Stone is of one Nature and one Thing. And Rosinus says: Be assured that there is only one thing from which everything you desire is made. And *Lilium*: You need no more than one thing, which transforms into another nature at every stage of our Work. Geber also says in his *Summa*: It is one Stone, one medicine <note>original: "Artney"</note>, to which we seek nothing to add, nor take anything away, but only separate the superfluous from it. And Scites in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum*, or "Assembly of the Sages," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note> says: the foundation of this art is a single thing, which is stronger and higher than all things, and is called the sharp vinegar <note>original: "scharffe Essig"; likely referring to a potent solvent or the "mercurial water" used to dissolve metals.</note>, which has caused the Gold to become a pure spirit, without which neither the Whiteness, nor the Blackness, nor the Redness <note>The three main stages of the alchemical work: albedo, nigredo, and rubedo.</note> can exist: and when it is mixed with the Body, it is retained, and becomes one with it, and transforms it into a spirit, and colors it with a spiritual and unchangeable color, and receives from the colored thing in return its bodily color, which cannot be extinguished: and if you were to set the body to the fire without vinegar, it would burn.

<margin>Scitis.</margin>
But someone might conclude from these words of Scites that there are not one, but two things required—namely the Body and the Vinegar, as he calls it—and that one must necessarily join a Wet and a Dry together, so that the dry is not burned by the fire, but protected from such burning by the Wet. To such an argument and conclusion I must rightly agree, if it is only understood correctly, and nonetheless maintain the dignity and truth of the aforementioned Philosophical sayings. For it is certain that there is only one single material <note>original: "Materia"</note> of our blessed Stone, which has many names among the Sages, which Nature prepares for the artist, and has ordained as the material of the Great Stone alone, and otherwise no other thing in the world.

<margin>Paracelsus in the book on the Tincture of the Philosophers.</margin>
This material is before everyone's eyes; the whole world sees it, grasps it, loves it, and yet does not know it: It is noble and common, expensive and cheap, precious and lowly, and is found in all places. **Theophrastus Paracelsus** calls it in his book *Tinctura Physicorum* <note>The Tincture of the Natural Philosophers</note>, the Red Lion, named by many, known by few. Hermes calls it in the 1st Chapter of his book Mercury, which is hardened in the innermost chambers. In the *Turba* it is called here and there Bronze or Ore: in the *Rosarium Philosophorum* <note>The Rosary of the Philosophers</note> it is called Salt. In short, this Material has as many names as there are things in the world. Therefore it also happens that it is so little understood by the ignorant. I call them ignorant because they proceed to the Art without prior knowledge of Nature and its properties, like a beast going to the manger, not knowing what it is stretching its mouth toward, as Arnoldus says.

<margin>Geber.</margin>
Therefore **Geber** says well and rightly in his *Summa Perfectionis*: He who does not know the beginnings of Nature within himself is still far removed from this Art. <margin>Rosarius.</margin> And **Rosarius** says: I advise that no one undertake to find this Art unless he recognizes the beginning of true Nature and its order: When he has recognized it, he needs no more than a single thing, and it does not require great expense: For it is no more than one Stone, one medicine, one vessel, one order, and one arrangement. Thus our Material is separated through the help of Nature and the artist's clever maneuvers so that, as <margin>Theophrast.</margin> **Theophrastus** says, it is transformed into the White Eagle <note>An alchemical term often referring to a purified or sublimated state of the matter.</note>, so that the brilliance of the Sun <note>original: "Solis"; gold.</note> does not outshine the <term>Spagyrist</term> <note>A practitioner of spagyrics, the branch of alchemy involving the separation and recombination of plant or mineral components.</note>, or (as <margin>Basil: Valent.</margin> **Basilius Valentinus** calls it) from it comes a spirit white as snow, and another spirit red as blood, both of which spirits have the third hidden within them.
