Therefore <margin>Aros Rex.</margin> King **Aros** does not speak ill when he says: Our medicine is made of two things of one essence, namely from the union of the constant, spiritual and bodily, cold and moist, warm and dry nature, and can be made from no other thing. <margin>Richard: Angl.</margin> And **Richardus Anglicus** says: It is one Stone, and one medicine, which the Philosophers have called Rebis <note>original: "Rebis"; a Latin term for 'a double thing,' representing the union of male/female or spirit/body.</note>, that is, from two things, namely from the Body and the Spirit, White or Red: in which many fools have erred in different ways, interpreting the verse: *The Rebis is the most correct rule in the stated figures.* <note>original: "Est rebis in dictis rectissima norma figuris."</note> That is, two things: and these two things are one thing, namely the Water that is added to the Body, and dissolves it into a spirit, that is, into a Mineral Water from which it was originally made, and thus from the Body and Spirit becomes a Mineral Water, which is called Elixir, that is, a Ferment <note>original: "Fermentum"</note>. For then the Water and the Spirit are one thing, from which the Tincture and medicine are made, which purify all bodies. Therefore our medicine is completed from one thing, which is the Water and Spirit of the Body. And so we have, according to the Philosophers, the nature of Sulfur and Mercury above the earth, from which Gold and Silver were made under the earth. <margin>Bernhardus.</margin> And **Bernhard, Count of Trevisan and the March** says: our work is made from one root and two Mercurial substances, taken raw and drawn from the mine, clean and pure, etc. <margin>Basilius Valent.</margin> And **Basilius Valentinus** says in the book of *Natural and Supernatural Things* in the 4th Chapter: I will reveal this to you in truth and through the love of God, that the root of the Philosophical Sulfur, which is a heavenly spirit, together with the root of the spiritual supernatural Mercury, as well as the beginning of the spiritual Salt, are in one, and are found in one Material, from which the Stone that was before me is made, and not in many things: Although Mercury for itself, and Sulfur for itself, along with Salt in particular, are cited by all Philosophers—that Mercury is found in one, Sulfur in one, and Salt in one—I tell you nevertheless that this only refers to their abundance, whichever is found most in each, and can be used and prepared particularly in many useful ways for medicine and the transformation of metals: But the Universal, as the highest treasure of earthly wisdom and the beginning of all three things, is a single thing, and is found and extracted from a single thing at once, which can make all metals into one, and is the true Spirit of Mercury and the Soul of Sulfur <note>original: "Anima Sulphuris"</note> together with the spiritual Salt, united together, enclosed under one heaven, and dwelling in one body, and is the Dragon and the Eagle, it is the King and the Lion, it is the Spirit and the Corpse, which must dye the corpse of Gold into a medicine, etc. Thus our prepared Material is called the Husband and the Wife.

<margin>Simon in the Turba.</margin>
Item, the active and the passive, as **Simon in the Turba** says: know that the secret of this work consists of the Man and the Woman, that is, of the active and the passive. In Lead is the Man, in Orpiment <note>original: "Auripigment"; arsenic trisulfide, used as a yellow pigment and in alchemical trials.</note> is the Woman. The Man rejoices in his accepted Woman, and is helped by her: and the Woman receives from the Man a coloring seed, and is colored by him. <margin>Diamedes.</margin> And **Diamedes** says: Join the male son of the red servant to his fragrant wife, and they will be so joined together to give birth to the Art, to which you shall introduce no stranger, nor powder, nor any other thing, and let the conception suffice for you, so the right son will be born to you: O how exceedingly precious is the material of this red servant, without which no order can exist: Others call it *Argentum vivum* <note>Quicksilver or Mercury.</note> or Mercury and Sulfur or Fire. <margin>Roger: Baco.</margin> As **Roger Bacon** says in the *Mirror* <note>original: "Speculo"</note>, chapter 3: From Sulfur and Mercury all metals are born, and nothing clings to them, nor is anything added to them, nor does anything change them, except what comes from them. Therefore we must also rightly take Mercury and Sulfur for the material of the Stone. And **Menabadus**: He who joins the quicksilver to the body of Magnesia, and the wife to the husband, draws out the hidden nature with which the bodies are colored.

<column-break/>

<margin>Lullius in his testament.</margin>
And **Lullius in his Codicil**: The property of our Mercury is that it allows itself to be coagulated by its Sulfur. And in the *Practice* of his *Testament* he says: The quicksilver is an overflowing and flowing moisture, which preserves against burning. Others call it the Body, Spirit, and Soul. <margin>Arnold. in the Flower of Flowers.</margin> Thus says **Arnoldus in the *Flower of Flowers*** <note>original: "flore florum"</note>: The Philosophers have said that our Stone is composed of the Body, the Soul, and the Spirit, and they have spoken the truth: For they compared the imperfect corpus to the Body, because it is weak: The Water they called the Spirit, and with truth, because it is a spirit: But the Ferment they spoke of with the name Soul, because it gives life to the imperfect body, which it did not have before, and brings forth a better form. And a little before he says: the spirit is not joined to its body except through the mediation of the soul: For the soul is the medium between the body and the spirit, which joins the two together. <margin>Morienus.</margin> And **Morienus** says: The soul enters its body swiftly: But if you wanted to join it to another body, you would labor in vain. <margin>Lilium.</margin> And ***Lilium***: The soul, the body, and the spirit are together, and it is one thing, which has everything within itself, and to which nothing foreign is added. But what need is there to cite and explain all the names by which our Material is called here? We will be satisfied with these, as they are the most common and closest to our purpose. And after we have actually investigated where our Material must come from and be obtained, we want to consider the Solution, as the main part of the whole art, for a little while, and sharpen our understanding through that same consideration.

Regarding now the consideration and weighing of what our Material is, and where one must get it, it is to know that the Almighty Creator, whose wisdom is as great as He Himself, namely infinite, in the beginning, when there was nothing but Himself, created two kinds of things, namely the Heavenly ones, and those that are under the Heaven. The Heavenly ones are the Heavens themselves, and the Heavenly inhabitants, about whom we do not want to philosophize extensively at this time. The works created under the Heaven are those created from the four elements, and in their number only three kinds of genera are found: namely, first, everything that has life and feeling, and they are called *Animalia*; then everything that grows out of the earth and does not feel, and they are called *Vegetabilia*. Finally, everything that grows under the earth, and they are called *Mineralia*.

These three genera of creatures now encompass everything that was created from the four elements under the moon, and there are found neither more nor less of them; they are also confirmed by the highest God, each in its genus and its kind, so that none can be transformed from its kind and genus into that of another. As if one wanted to make a human or a tree out of a stone: or an ape or lead out of a herb: or another animal or herb out of lead. Such a thing, I say, is impossible by the decree of the Great King. For if such were permitted in Nature, their genera could be reduced, or indeed all turned into one. But because everything would then fall into a heap, the Lord of all Lords did not want to permit such a changing of the genera: And what is more, he did not only want to maintain these three genera each in its kind, but also to incorporate into each genus its own seed, so that each might thereby increase and remain in its form, and not have this form transformed into the form of another, such as a human into a horse: or an apple tree into lettuce: or a diamond or other stone into gold. Such a thing, I say, is not permitted in the nature of the lower things. And as it has been from the beginning, so it will remain until the day when the Almighty, who said in the beginning, "Let it be," will say, "Let it pass away." But it is well permitted that among those things which have a common Material, seed, and composition of the elements, an ennobling and improvement of its state can be performed and completed, according to whether their material is pure and perfect.

As one sees that a human of much higher and more sensible mind rises much higher than others who do not have such sharp and subtle understanding, which then stems and springs from the pure and subtle spirits, as from the justified and well-tempered constitution of the body. Thus one also sees how one horse is much nobler than another, and so nearly in all species of animals. Now as this behaves in the animal genera, so it is also found abundantly in the herbs and trees. In the trees through planting, grafting, and other means known to diligent gardeners: in the herbs one sees daily how the herbs and flowers of one kind are each nobler, more beautiful, more fragrant, better, and tastier than the others: One only has to look at the gillyflowers <note>original: "Garoyphyllos"</note> or carnations and the tulips—I will not speak of others at this time—how many kinds of them there are, so that one can nearly not count them, which through diligent care and improvement become ever nobler, so that such beautiful and fragrant flowers come forth, the likes of which, it seems, have never grown before.

What shall I now say of the metals, whose general Material is Mercury, which is cooked and coagulated by Sulfur? As <margin>Richardus Angl. c. 6.</margin> **Richardus Anglicus** says in chapter 6: Nature has worked the genera of all liquid and meltable things from the essence of Mercury and its Sulfur: for the property of Mercury is that it allows itself to be coagulated by the vapor, as if by the heat of Sulfur, which is Red or White and does not burn. And <margin>Arnold. l. 2. c. 2.</margin> **Arnoldus** speaks in his first part in the 2nd Chapter: On the perfection of the <term>Magistery</term> <note>The completion of the alchemical Great Work.</note>: Mercury is the beginning of all things that can be melted, because all meltable things, when they are melted, are transformed into it, and it allows itself to be mixed with them because it is of their essence: although these same bodies are different from Mercury in their composition, in such a way according to whether it was pure or impure, from the impure Sulfur, which is foreign and contrary to it. And <margin>Rosinus ad Saratant.</margin> **Rosinus** says to **Saratanta**: The material of all metals is the cooked and imperfect Mercury, which Sulfur cooks in the belly of the earth: and as the Sulfur is different, so also different metals are born in the earth, because they all have one single and general initial Material together, only that more or less action comes to it or does not come to it, with moderation.

Therefore we see daily before our eyes how Nature herself strives in constant labor to purify them and bring them to more perfection, and to make them into Gold, which is Nature's final intention, as we then see in all metals that Nature has begun to work in them: since none is found so poor that it does not carry a grain of silver or gold with it, and indeed it is done with the metals thus, that Nature immediately wants to and can make Gold out of the Mercury that has its own Sulfur with it,

<meta>Catchword: Quicksilver-</meta>

<summary>This page explores the alchemical doctrine of the 'One Thing,' arguing that despite many names, the Stone's material is a single substance found in nature. It also outlines the hierarchy of the three kingdoms—animal, vegetable, and mineral—stating that while species cannot cross over, they can be 'ennobled' or perfected within their own kind, leading to the perfection of metals into gold.</summary>
<keywords>Prima Materia, Mercury, Sulfur, Salt, Paracelsus, Basilius Valentinus, Rebis, Three Kingdoms, Animalia, Vegetabilia, Mineralia, Ennoblement of Metals, Magistery</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the "One Thing" (the *prima materia*) of the Philosopher's Stone. The previous page established that the Stone is derived from a single source; this page explores the composition of metals—specifically the interplay between Mercury and Sulfur—and argues that the Stone must be sought within the Mineral kingdom, rejecting animal and vegetable sources as unsuitable.</meta>

if nothing foreign interferes, and the impure, stinking, and combustible Sulfur does not prevent it, just as we see that in many places pure and fine Gold is found without the admixture of other metals.

But because a foreign Sulfur commonly enters the veins of the earth alongside the Mercury, contaminating it and hindering its perfection, different metals are born according to the nature of this foreign Sulfur. As Aristotle says in the 4th book of *Meteorology*:

> When the essence of the Mercury is good, and the combustible Sulfur is impure, he transforms it into Copper. But if the Mercury is stony, impure, and earthy, and the Sulfur is also impure, Iron is made from it. It appears, however, that Tin has good Mercury, which is pure, but a bad and poorly mixed Sulfur. Lead, however, has a coarse, bad, heavy, and loamy Mercury, and a bad, tasteless, and stinking Sulfur, which is why it does not readily allow itself to be coagulated <note>To "coagulate" in alchemy means to solidify or fix a volatile substance into a stable form.</note>.

This hindering, combustible, and stinking Sulfur, however, is not the true fire which fully "cooks" the metals; rather, Mercury has its own internal Sulfur which performs this task, as Bernard of Trevisan <note>original: "Bernhardus Graue von Trygene"</note> says:

> Many vainly imagine that a sulfurous material enters into the birth of metals; but it is, on the contrary, evident that when Nature works, Mercury’s own Sulfur is enclosed within it. This Sulfur does not rule within it except through the warm movement, by which the said Sulfur, and simultaneously the other two properties of Mercury, are changed: and in this way, different metals are born in the various wombs of the earth. For in metals, as Arnaldus de Villa Nova <note>original: "Arnoldus"</note> says in the 1st Chapter of his *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarij"; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>, there is a twofold superfluity: One is hidden and locked within the innermost essence of the Mercury itself, which mixed into it at the beginning of its being; the other, however, comes from outside its nature and corrupts it. And while this latter type can be separated with great effort, the former cannot be removed by the ingenuity of any artist.

Therefore, the great heat of the fire separates the combustible moisture from the metals, because the Mercury retains that which is of its own nature and protects it from burning, but thrusts away the foreign matter and lets it burn. The internal Sulfur, however, which cooks the Mercury and matures it to perfection, is likewise pure and impure, combustible and incombustible: The combustible prevents the Mercury from reaching its perfection, so that it cannot become Gold until such sulfur is finally separated from it, and only the pure, incombustible, and constant Sulfur remains; then it becomes Gold or Silver, depending on whether such Sulfur is white or red. This internal Sulfur is nothing other than a "mature" Mercury, or the most ripe and seasoned part of the Mercury, which is why the Mercury accepts it so gladly, but leaves foreign Sulfur aside. As Richard says in chapter 9:

> The simpler and purer the Sulfur is, the more it rejoices in simple and pure Mercury, and clings to it, so that one is more strongly joined with the other, and thus more perfect metals are born from them.

Such Sulfur, however, is not found above the earth, as Avicenna <note>original: "Auicenna"</note> says, except for what is found in the two bodies, namely Gold and Silver, and indeed it is much more mature in Gold. For, as Richard says in chapter 12:

> The red Sulfur is in Gold through greater maturation <note>original: "zeitigung"</note>, and the white is in Silver through lesser maturation.

If all this is so—namely, that there is one single and universal material of metals, which becomes Gold (the end of all metals and the perfect intention of Nature) either quickly through the power of its innate Sulfur, or over a long time by separating itself from the foreign, useless Sulfur through constant "cooking"—then we must confess and say that Nature, in this kingdom as well, seeks and desires its own improvement and perfection within its own nature, according to the purity and subtlety of the subject, just as in the Vegetable and Animal kingdoms.

I have wished to indicate this a bit more thoroughly, dear Seeker of Natural Things, so that you may more fundamentally understand and grasp from where you must obtain and take the material <note>term: "materia" — the raw substance used to begin the alchemical work.</note> of our Great Stone. For if you should attempt to make our Stone from an animal material, you will find your way blocked, because the two belong to two different kingdoms: since the Stone is Mineral, but the material would be Animal. And from nothing—as Richard says in chapter 1—can something be extracted that is not already within it. Therefore, because every species seeks the increase of its natural power within its own species, and every genus within its genus, and every nature within its own nature brings forth fruit according to its own properties and in no other nature contrary to it: therefore, every seed agrees with its own kind. And Basil Valentine <note>original: "Basilius Valentinus"</note> says:

> Observe and understand, my friend, that you should not choose any animal soul for this work. Your flesh and blood, as granted and given by the Creator to animals, belongs to the animals, from which God built them together so that an animal might come of it.

Therefore, one must marvel at those who consider themselves great artists and yet seek their material for the Stone in menstrual blood, in semen, in eggs, in hair, in urine, and other such things, filling many books with such recipes and deceiving and misleading others who are less knowledgeable with such worthless things.

And Roger Bacon <note>original: "Rogerius Baccho"</note> marvels at the folly of such people in the 3rd chapter of the *Mirror* <note>original: "Speculi"; likely referring to the *Speculum Alchemiae*.</note>, where he says:

> It is therefore to be wondered at that a prudent man bases his intention on animal and vegetable things, which are far removed, when minerals can be found which are much closer.

It is also by no means to be believed that any Philosopher placed the art in the aforementioned far-removed things, unless it were by way of allegory. Thus, our Stone—as Basil Valentine says—does not point toward things that are combustible. For our Stone and its material are safe from all danger of fire. Therefore, you may well cease looking in animal things, since Nature has not permitted it to be found in them. Should someone wish to seek our Stone in vegetable matters, such as in trees, herbs, or flowers, they will err no less than the one who wants to make a great rock from an animal. For all herbs and trees, along with everything that comes from them, are combustible, and nothing remains behind but a mere salt with its earth, which it received from Nature in its composition. And let no one be misled by those who claim to make the Philosophers' Stone from wheat; much less listen to those who undertake to make this Stone from wine and its dregs <note>referring to tartar or lees.</note>. For because they do not rightly understand the writings of Raymond Lully <note>original: "Raymundi Lullij"</note>, they prove with their "great understanding" that they understand nothing, misleading both themselves and others. It is true that very magnificent and excellent solvents <note>term: "Menstrua" — in alchemy, a solvent used to dissolve solids; not related to biology here.</note> can be prepared and readied from these, without which nothing noble can be accomplished in either Medicine or Alchemy—

<column-break/>

But that the Stone of the Wise could be made from it, or its seed drawn from it, has not been permitted to Nature by the Creator of all things; rather, as mentioned above, it is commanded to remain within its own kind.

Therefore, every sensible person must easily conclude and decide that, since our Stone is incombustible, as has been said, and animal and vegetable things are all combustible, it must be sought and made from an incombustible material, which is found nowhere else but in the Mineral kingdom.

Since our aforementioned Philosophic Stone is a mineral work, one rightly asks—because there are many kinds of minerals, such as stones (among which all sorts of materials and earths are understood), salts, middle minerals <note>term: "middle minerals" — substances like antimony or sulfur that were seen as occupying a space between salts and true metals.</note>, and metals—from which of these must it finally be made?

To this I answer: it is not possible to make it from stones, because there is no fluid or meltable Mercury in them, or they cannot be melted, dissolved, and brought into their first material because of their excess of coarse Sulfur and the abundance of their earthy property. Likewise, no wise researcher of natural secrets will seek such material for the Philosophic Stone in salts, alums, and similar things. For in these, he will see nothing other than a sharp, corrosive, and destructive spirit, but will find no Mercury or Sulfur at all, such as the Philosophers require and desire.

Furthermore, no "middle mineral," such as Magnesia, Marcasite, Antimony, etc., can be made from such things. Much less can a metal come from them: how then could the material of the Philosophic Stone be taken from them, which is the end and perfection of all metals and mineral things? In addition, such things have absolutely no fellowship or friendship with metals; instead, they burn, break, and corrupt them—how then can they serve for their improvement? Hear only what Richard the Englishman <note>original: "Richardus Anglicus"</note> says of this in chapter 10:

> The lesser minerals cannot become metals, firstly because they are not born from the first material of metals, which is Mercury.

Because their birth is distinguished from the birth of Mercury from the start—in form, material, and composition—no metals can come from them, since there is only one single first material and seed for any single thing from which it is born. What has been said appears clearly from this: the lesser minerals are not born from Mercury, as is evident from Aristotle and Avicenna. Therefore, if metals were to be made from them, they would first have to be brought back into the first material.

Since this cannot happen through any art, they also cannot be metals, and consequently cannot be the material of the Stone. Furthermore, because the lesser minerals cannot be the beginning of the art through art (which is Mercury), they also cannot reach the middle and the end (which are the metals and the Tincture). Instead, because the lesser minerals are foreign to the metallic nature—even if they share in some mineral power—they are of weaker virtue and are combustible. Therefore, the metallic nature does not rejoice in them at all, but thrusts them away, and keeps only what is of its own nature. Thus, they are fools who bring to light so many and various works and deceptions to cheat people—namely, absurd things which Nature neither contains nor can provide.

Let no one be led astray by the writings of the Philosophers when they occasionally speak of salts, as when it is said in the *Allegories of the Sages*:

> He who works without salt will not raise the dead corpses.

And in the book *Soliloquies* it is written:

> He who works without salt shoots with a bow without a string.

For they have a much different meaning than that of mineral salts. This is clearly seen in the *Rosarium Philosophorum* when it says:

> The Salt of the metals is the Philosophic Stone.

For our Stone is a "coagulated water" in Gold or Silver, and it resists fire, and is dissolved in its own water, from which it was made according to its kind. That the "coagulated water" of the Philosophers is not mineral water, but their Mercury, Geber teaches in the *Book of Furnaces* <note>original: "Buche von dem Ofen"</note> in the 19th chapter, where he says:

> Diligently apply yourself, Son, to dissolving the dry water of the Sun and Moon <note>symbolism: "Sun" is Gold, "Moon" is Silver.</note>, which the common man calls Mercury.

The Philosophers also sometimes call their speeches "Salt," as seen in the *Clangor Buccinae* <note>The "Sound of the Trumpet," another classic alchemical text.</note>, where it is said:

> Note that the bodies are Alum and Salt, which flow from our bodies.

Sometimes they even call the Medicine itself "Salt," as it stands in the *Scala* <note>referring to the *Scala Philosophorum* (The Philosophers' Ladder).</note>:

> The work of the second water is that it heats the earth into its wonderful Salt, solely through its attractive power.

And Arnaldus de Villa Nova says in the book *On the Preservation of Youth*:

> But that which has no equal in the preservation of youth is the Salt from the mine <note>original: "Saltz auß der Minera"</note>.

The wise, when it was prepared, compared it to the natural warmth of a healthy youth, and because of this comparison, they named the Stone with the names of animals, or other mineral ciphers, and some called it an everlasting medicine and "Water of Life" <note>original: "Aquam Vitæ"</note>. The entire science of its preparation is that it be reduced into a pure and drinkable water, with those things that have the same property as itself.

From this it is now clearly seen that neither according to the teaching of the Philosophers nor the property of Nature itself can the material of the Stone be taken from the lesser minerals.

Now we want to look around a bit to see if the material of our Great Stone can be made from the "middle minerals," such as Marcasites, Antimony, Magnesia, and others, especially since the Philosophers mention them several times. As when Senior says:

> If the power to coagulate Mercury were not in Orpiment <note>original: "Auripigment"</note>, our mastery would never reach its end.

And Thomas Aquinas:

> Take our Antimony, or the hooked black earth, etc.

And Parmenides in the *Turba*:

> Take Mercury and coagulate it in the body of Magnesia, or in the Sulfur that does not burn.

Yet it must be known that the Philosophers do not speak this way with the intention of suggesting that our Great Stone can be made from such things; rather, they speak only by way of allegory. For the Orpiment and Magnesia of the Philosophers is a much different thing than the common ones—namely, it is the material itself, which they otherwise call the Agent, the Lion, the King, the Sulfur, and by many more names. It is called Orpiment because it has the power to turn Gold into an abundant color, and it is called Magnesia because of its great virtue and glory which springs and flows from it.

But that Thomas Aquinas calls it Antimony, he does so because of the weak, shimmering color it assumes after dissolution. For when our Stone has become black, the Philosophers compared it to all black things.

Someone might, however, argue and say to me that some of these middle minerals are not only born from Mercury and Sulfur, but even become metals: as one sees that Magnesia or Bismuth mixes itself with Lead and Tin.

<sig>X 2</sig>

<vocab>Alchemy, Philosophers' Stone, Mercury, Sulfur, Magnesia, Antimony, Orpiment, Marcasite, Quicksilver, Gold, Silver, Salt of metals, Bismuth, Water of Life, Solvents</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that the Philosopher's Stone must be sought within the Mineral kingdom, specifically through the purification of Mercury and its internal Sulfur. The author systematically rejects animal and vegetable sources as "combustible" and unsuitable, clarifying that alchemical references to "salt," "eggs," or "blood" are merely allegorical ciphers for metallic processes.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury-Sulfur theory, maturation of metals, mineral kingdom, alchemical allegories, rejection of organic matter, Bernard of Trevisan, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Basil Valentine, Roger Bacon</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the true "Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone. The previous page discussed how metals are formed from Mercury and Sulfur in the earth. This page systematically evaluates and rejects various substances—Antimony, Vitriol, common Sulfur, and common Mercury—as the primary material for the Stone, arguing that the true "seed" must be found within a specific metallic root.</meta>

Furthermore, **Antimony** <note>original: "Antimonium"</note> does not only mix with metals, but it also naturally turns into Lead: many persons of high and low standing have also seen Gold come from it. Since these substances are born from **Sulfur** and **Mercury**—into which they can also be **reduced** again through art—and share the same origin as the metals, could the **Matter** of the Stone be taken from them? To this I answer: first, a distinction should rightly be held among these intermediate minerals—namely, between those that contain **Mercury** and those that do not. The first group, those containing **Mercury**, should be carefully considered, because their **Mercury** can be transformed into Gold and Silver by our **Medicine**; therefore, in my opinion, they should be regarded as half-metals that have the **disposition** to become metals. The others, however, which have no **Mercury**, are worth nothing. But because of the evil and combustible sulfur found in them—which is the reason **Antimony** is the enemy of all metals and burns them, except for Gold, which it must leave in peace because of its constancy—they cannot reach that level. Therefore, one cannot choose them as the **Matter** of our Stone, which must be a pure, perfect **Mercury** and a pure, delicate, and incombustible Sulfur. That they are entirely impure and completely **infected** by their sulfur is easily seen when one brings them into their "school" and examines what they have learned.

For one sees in **Zinc**, which by its external appearance and feel—its shine and weight—one would take for pure **Mercury**, that as soon as it enters the fire, it quickly flies away in smoke like a pale yellow sulfur. The **Marcasites** <note>Iron pyrites or similar sparkling minerals.</note> cannot be forced to melt by any means because of their great earthy impurity. **Antimony**, however, which can be cleansed of its excessive blackness by clever handling and brought into a very white and beautiful **Regulus** <note>A "regulus" is the metallic heart or button of a mineral left after smelting.</note>, has the appearance above all others as if something special could be made from it; indeed, many who otherwise consider themselves wise are of the opinion that the Philosopher's Stone must be made from it. But despite all this, though **Antimony** be cleansed of its blackness as much as possible, its ill-nature and brittle sulfurousness remain with it; this is apparent from the fact that it does not allow itself to be stretched under the hammer or become malleable, which is a property of every metal by which it is recognized as such alongside other qualities.

Beyond this, it contains a coarse, impure **Mercury**, not to mention that it always retains a volatile sulfur. And those who consider themselves great Philosophers—and who try to persuade others through many books and extensive writings that this very substance is the "Sample" of their **Universal** <note>The "Universal" refers to the Alkahest or the one-size-fits-all starting material for the Stone.</note>—must kindly forgive me that I cannot agree with their opinion. For one seeks quite foolishly for something in a thing that is not there, as **Arnoldus** says: and because it is written in the *Practice of the Turba* <note>Referring to the "Turba Philosophorum," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note> that the Philosopher's Stone is a pure **Matter**; so also says **Lullius** <note>Raymund Lully, a famous 13th-century polymath and reputed alchemist.</note> in his last testament: "Our Tincture is nothing but a pure fire." And in the *Vade Mecum* he says: "It is only the subtle spirit that **tinctures** <note>To "tincture" means to transform the internal nature and color of a base metal into gold.</note> and cleanses the **Bodies** of their leprosy." But minerals, like the others, are so coarse and impure that they cannot be cured of this or internally cleansed by any means, unless it happens through our Tincture. Therefore, the **Matter** of our Stone cannot be obtained from them: since from nothing, as **Richardus** says in **chapter 1**, can something be taken that is not already inside it.

But what should one say of **Vitriol** <note>Metal sulfates, often green (iron) or blue (copper).</note>, which through its wonderful properties also leads many into error—especially since a large part of it turns into Copper, and it can even transform Iron into Copper? Regarding this, one should briefly know that **Vitriol** is nothing other than a beginning and **Matter** of Copper. In the veins of the earth, where the mountain vapors and misty **Mercury** encountered a place where such a bitter, constricting, Venusian **Sulfur** <note>Alchemists associated Copper with the planet Venus.</note> was found in great quantity, the Mercury immediately accepted it, **coagulated** within it, and sought to become a metal. But because Nature wanted to separate the pure from the impure, the combustible from the incombustible, yet the mass of the aforementioned sulfur far exceeded the **quantity** of the Mercury, the **Mercury** had to be separated during this division and hide itself within the Vitriolic greenness.

This is clearly seen in the fact that if one adds common sulfur (as the cause of this corruption) to Copper and **calcinates** it—for Art does with a strong fire in a short time what Nature must perform with a slow fire—it burns the Copper entirely and brings it into a Vitriolic state through common manual operations. Depending on whether there is much or little sulfur, the Vitriol becomes richer or poorer in color. From this it comes that one Vitriol has more of a copper-like quality than another; for instance, in Cyprian vitriol one finds much copper, in others less.

It is also well to note that the acidic **Spirit**, which is in Vitriol, originates from the sulfur, since one finds and extracts the same from common sulfur. Thus, one can perceive the sulfurous smell in the **Spirit of Vitriol**, and the **Spirit of Sulfur** also transforms the **Sulfur of Mars** <note>Iron sulfur.</note> into a Vitriol, just like the **Spirit of Vitriol**. Because there is also a coarse **Sulfur** in Iron, the sharpness of the Vitriol eats it away, seeks its **Mercury** (which is not very different from its own), and through the union of its sulfur—which overcomes the **Mercury**—it becomes good, malleable Copper.

Since there is such an abundant and harsh sulfur in Vitriol, and so very little **Mercury** (which has not yet reached its purification), we shall obtain no more from it than from the others. And we must heed the teaching of **Alphidius** in all these matters, who says: "My son, beware and separate yourself from the dead bodies and stones, for there is no path to walk therein, since their life is not increased but consumed; such as the salts, **Orpiments, Arsenic, Magnesia, Marcasite**, and the like."

And **Arnoldus** says in *The Flower of Flowers* <note>original: "flore florum"</note>: "The cause of their error is because the four spirits—namely Orpiment, Sal ammoniac, **Mercury**, and **Sulfur**—are not the seed of either the perfect or imperfect metals: except for the **Mercury** and **Sulfur** which **coagulate** the Mercury."

But someone might conclude from these last words of **Arnoldus** that common **Sulfur** and **Mercury** are the **Matter** of our Stone, because they are counted among the four Spirits and because **Sulfur coagulates Mercury**? To this I must ask, with **Richardus** in **chapter 1**, whether *every* sulfur coagulates **Mercury**? To that I answer: No. For every common sulfur, as the **Philosopher** says, is contrary to the metals. For it is known that sulfur is born from the fatness of the earth in the crevices of the ground and is made hard by moderate cooking, and then it is called sulfur.

But Sulfur is of two kinds: living and combustible. The living is the active part of the metals, and when it is cleansed of all impurity by Nature, it is the **Matter** of our Stone, of which more hereafter.

<column-break/>

The combustible and common sulfur, however, is not the **Matter** of the metals or the **Stone**, but rather their enemy. For common and combustible sulfur does not enter into our mastery, say **Avicenna** and **Richardus Anglicus**, because it did not spring from there; it always infects, blackens, and destroys, in whatever way it may be prepared by Art: for it is a destroying fire.

Therefore, it hinders fluidity when it is made fixed. We see an example of this in Iron, which has a constant, coarse, and impure sulfur within it. When that is burned, an earthy essence comes from it, like a dead powder. How could this give life to others? For it has a twofold excess: namely, that which allows itself to be ignited, and the earthy part.

From this, consider now common sulfur, and not that of the Philosophers, which is a unique, living fire that makes other dead bodies alive and ripens them, etc. Therefore, common sulfur cannot be the **Matter** of our Stone. But what shall we say of common quicksilver? Since all Philosophers say that the **Matter** of our Stone is a **Mercurial substance**, and it has many properties attributed to our quicksilver: for it is the beginning of all things that can be melted, as **Arnoldus** says in *The Rosary*, **book 1, chapter 2**: "For all meltable things, when they are melted, are turned into it, and it mixes with them because it is of their **substance**": although the bodies in their composition are different from quicksilver, depending on whether it has been left pure or impure by its foreign sulfur. And in **chapter 4** he says: "**Mercury vive** <note>Living mercury/Quicksilver.</note> is clearly, in all its effects, the most perfect and constant thing: for it protects from burning, and causes flow when it is made fixed, and is the tincture for redness, of overflowing perfection, of shimmering light, and does not depart from the mixture as long as it lasts: and it is friendly and pleasing, and the means to join the tinctures together, because it allows itself to be mixed through and through with them, and clings to them internally, since it is of their nature. It alone overcomes the fire, but is not overcome by the fire, but rather rejoices in it and rests within it."

And **Bernhardus** <note>Bernard of Trevisan.</note> says: "We follow Nature most exactly, which in its ore veins has no other **Matter** in which it works than the pure **Mercurial form**." In this **Mercury** lies the constant and incombustible Sulfur, which completes our work without any other **substance** than the pure **Mercurial substance**. If such magnificent virtues are in **Mercury**, does it not necessarily follow that such must be the **Matter** of our Stone? To this we answer that, just as there are two kinds of sulfur, so also are there two kinds of **Mercury**: the common and the Philosophical. Common **Mercury** is still a raw, unripe, and open **Body** which does not remain in the fire like the Philosophical one, but is turned into smoke by gentle heat and driven away: therefore the Philosophers commonly say, "Our quicksilver is not common quicksilver." So also says **Lullius** in his *Clavicula*, **chapter 1**: "We say that common quicksilver cannot be the quicksilver of the Philosophers, with whatever art it may be prepared: for the common cannot be kept in the fire, unless it happens through another bodily quicksilver that is warm, dry, and more ripe."

But the majority of Philosophers, according to their abundance, have written about the **sublimation** <note>Turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it.</note> and other preparations of common quicksilver; from this, so many wonderful labors have arisen, and the nature and property of this **Subject** have been learned all the more. But the goal they aimed for—namely, the great treasure of earthly wisdom—has never yet been found therein, because Nature did not place it there. In truth, it appears so wondrous in its operation that it could mislead one who claims to be wise: for example, I knew someone who had **amalgamated** it with Gold and handled it so subtly that he brought it through all the colors, even into **Citrinity** <note>A yellow color, often the final stage before the "Red" completion of the Stone.</note>.

But when it would not change further from that color, and he gave it a stronger fire, thinking it was now fixed—where, according to the Philosophers, one could not go wrong with the fire in that manner—the glass burst. The **Mercury** flew up the chimney, carrying the Gold with it, and gilded the chimney itself. From this, they nevertheless scraped together much Gold and **reduced** it again.

Thus it was seen that common **Mercury**, being itself a **Body**, could neither release nor work within another **Body** (namely Gold), even though many colors appeared because the heat worked upon the moisture. Had this good man, like many others, observed what **Arnoldus** says in *The Flower of Flowers*, he would not have proceeded in such a manner. For thus speaks **Arnoldus**, talking of such laborers: "When they looked at this more subtly, they considered that quicksilver was the beginning and origin of metals; with the sulfurous and boiling heat they **sublimated** the **Mercury** for themselves, thereafter they **fixed** it, they opened it and **coagulated** it, but when they came to the **projection** <note>The final act of throwing the Stone onto base metal to transform it into gold.</note>, they found nothing, etc."

Therefore, we cannot regard common quicksilver as the **Matter** of our Stone either. We have hitherto sought the **Matter** of our Stone in **Animals**, in **Vegetables**, in stones, in the lesser **minerals**, and even in the intermediate and greater minerals, but we have still not found it. We must therefore look further to see if we can find it in the metals, and if it should be there, whether it is in all of them at once, or only in some, and in which it is to be met. For it is known, and **Rogerius Baco** <note>Roger Bacon, 13th-century philosopher and scientist.</note> also testifies in his *Speculum*, **chapter 3**, that all **Metals** are born of Sulfur and Quicksilver, and that nothing can be attached or added to them, nor anything transformed, except that which comes from them. Since every improvement increases a thing's nature from which it is made, as **Richardus** says in **chapter 1**, and otherwise in all of Nature it is ordained by the highest Creator that every thing should bring forth and bear its like, and that a horse is not born of a man. And just as irrational animals cannot bring forth their like for any increase except through the nature of their like: so you must not, says **Basilius Valentinus** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist.</note>, seek or undertake to make the true Stone except from its own seed, from which our Stone itself was made from the beginning.

But to find this seed, you must consider within yourself what you desire to seek our Stone for; then it will become known to you that it must flow from nowhere else than from a **Metallic root**, from which the metals themselves were ordained by the Creator to be born. Thus there is a great similarity in the birth of the metals and of the Great Stone, since in both, the Sulfur and Quicksilver—which have the Salt as the noble Soul hidden within them—are necessarily required. And it is not possible to attain the salvation of utility in **Metallic form** until these three, taken from **Metallic essence**, have been brought together into one; for nothing must be added to the metals that does not have its origin from them.

<summary>This page argues that common minerals like antimony, zinc, and vitriol—as well as common sulfur and mercury—cannot be the starting material for the Philosopher's Stone because of their impurities and lack of metallic malleability. The author concludes that the "seed" of the Stone must be sought within a specific metallic root containing the triad of Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt.</summary>
<keywords>Antimony, Vitriol, Zinc, Sulfur, Mercury, Philosopher's Stone, Arnold of Villanova, Raymund Lully, alchemy, transmutation, metallic root, three principles</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the true "Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone. The previous page discussed how metals are formed from Mercury and Sulfur in the earth and rejected substances like Antimony and common Zinc. This page moves deeper into the debate among ancient masters about whether the Stone should be made from imperfect metals (like Lead or Copper) or only from the perfect metals (Gold and Silver).</meta>

Thus it is manifest, as Bacchus says, that no other thing—if it does not take its origin from **Mercury** and **Sulfur**—is sufficient to make the same perfect and to bring about its transformation. Therefore, it is necessary to take a metallic material for the improvement of the **Great Stone** <note>The "Great Stone" refers to the final Philosopher's Stone capable of transmuting metals.</note>: but whether such a thing is to be found in the imperfect metals, we shall examine with a few words.

Four of these are found who wish to take the *Material of the Stone* <note>original: "Materiam Lapidis"</note> from **Tin** or **Lead** for the white work, and for the red from **Copper** or **Iron**, or from both of them together; they are, without doubt, misled by the Philosophers themselves. For thus speaks **Geber** <note>A 13th-century alchemist, often cited as the father of chemistry</note> in his *Book of Furnaces*, Chapter 9: "The dough <note>the alchemical mass</note> which one must ferment, we draw, as is customary, from the imperfect bodies. And therefore we give you a common rule: that the white dough is drawn from **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> and **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, but the red is drawn from **Venus** <note>Copper</note>, **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, and **Mars** <note>Iron</note>." Likewise, **Basilius Valentinus** teaches in his book *Of Natural and Supernatural Things* to make a **Tincture** <term>Tincture: A concentrated medicinal or transmuting substance</term> from the **Conjunction** of **Mars** <note>Iron</note> and **Venus** <note>Copper</note>.

Item, in the *Triumphal Chariot*, he speaks thus: "following this is the **Tincture of the Sun and Moon** <note>Gold and Silver</note> etc. upon white; further, the **Tincture of Vitriol** or **Venus**, likewise the **Tincture of Mars**, both of which also contain the **Tincture of the Sun** within themselves, if they have previously been brought to a constant **Fixation** <term>Fixation: The process of making a substance stable in fire</term>. These are followed by the **Tincture of Jupiter and Saturn** for the **Coagulation of Mercury**, and thereafter the **Tincture of Mercury** itself."

But the researcher of natural secrets should know that this was not the meaning of **Geber** and **Basilius Valentinus** or other Philosophers; otherwise, they would speak against themselves, which cannot be, since the Sages in their writings must never lie, even if they hide the truth in veiled speech. For at once, neither in the imperfect metals alone, nor mixed among one another, can any perfection spring forth by which they might be improved in the least. From themselves alone such a thing cannot be, because the purest essence of **Mercury** is required for the *Material of our Stone*, as *The Sound of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor Buccinae"</note>, **Avicenna**, **Lull**, and in general all Philosophers say: "We must choose for our work the purest Mercurial substance." This purest substance of **Mercury**, however, is not found in the imperfect metals by nature, because they are so corrupted and occupied by their foreign and impure **Sulfur** that they, like leprous bodies, cannot be brought to internal and perfect cleansing by any art. They also do not remain stable in the fire, which is a necessary property of our material—namely, that it remains constant in the fire, which does not happen here.

Let us hear what **Geber** says in his *Summa*, Chapter 63, regarding this impurity of the imperfect metals and the property of the perfect **Mercury**: "through this, with true discovery, we have found a wonderful genus of two secrets: the first is that there are three causes for the corruption of every (imperfect) metal by fire. The first of these is that the combustible **Sulfur** flowed into their innermost essence and, ignited by strong fire, diminishes the entire essence of the body, drives it into smoke, and finally consumes it, no matter how good its **Quicksilver** <note>Mercury</note> may be."

"The second cause is that the external flame is increased by them, passes through them, and dissolves them into smoke, as they may be of themselves."

"The third cause is that their bodies can be failed through **Calcination** <term>Calcination: Heating a metal until it turns to a dry powder</term>: for then the flame of the fire can pass through them and drive them into smoke, no matter how perfect they are. Now, when all causes of corruption come together, the bodies must necessarily perish and come to nothing; if they are not together, the speed of the destruction of the bodies slackens somewhat. The other genus of this secret is the goodness which is considered because of the **Quicksilver** in the bodies: for the **Quicksilver**, because no causes of corruption and absorption are found within it, does not let itself be divided into parts of the composition, but remains with its entire essence in the fire; from which one must necessarily recognize the cause of its perfection. Therefore, let us praise the highly praised and highest God who created the same, and gave it the essence, and to the essence the property, which one may find from no other things in nature—that this perfection might be brought into it by some art—which we find therein in its *proximate power* <note>original: "potentia propinqua," meaning it is ready to be transformed</note>. For this is that which overcomes the fire, but is not overcome by the fire, but rather rests friendly within it and rejoices in it."

To these recounted words, **Geber** proves without fail that the material of our Stone cannot be in the imperfect metals, because they are impure in themselves and, when one wishes to clean them, they completely flee from the fire; our **Mercury**, however, because of its purity, is constant in the fire and nothing can harm it.

Now, as the imperfect metals alone are not the material of our Stone, they can even less provide the same when mixed together, seeing as through their mixing they do not become purer than they were alone before.

Moreover, a new confusion arises from it, which is entirely contrary to our intention; only a single material is required, as thought above. **Haly** testifies to this clearly when he says in the *Book of Secrets*, Chapter 9: "It is a Stone, among which you should mix no other thing: with this the wise work, and from this flows that which makes one healthy. Nothing else is mixed with it, neither in its whole nor in its parts."

And **Morienus** says: "This mastery stems initially from one root, which thereafter spreads out into more parts and is turned back again into its thing." If, then, the imperfect metals cannot be the material of the Stone, why did the Philosophers command us to work therein? Answer: When the Philosophers commanded taking the impure bodies, they did not mean thereby Copper, Iron, Lead, Tin, etc., but rather understood their *Body* or their *Earth*, as **Arnaldus** says in *The Flower of Flowers*: "**Mercury** is added to the Earth, that is, to the imperfect body." For although their Earth in and of itself is as perfect and pure as nature can make anything, it is still impure and imperfect *in respect to the physical stone* <note>original: "respectu lapidis Physici"</note>.

And herein Art surpasses Nature, because it performs that which Nature could not complete. But that this Earth, as said, is imperfect to us before perfect cleansing and rebirth, appears from the fact that it cannot yet **Tinct** <note>change the color or quality of other metals</note> and make perfect, and has no more than what nature has bestowed upon it: but when it is reborn, then it is capable of doing much. Its impurity is clearly to be felt in our work, in that it becomes entirely Black, and then is compared to **Lead** or **Antimony**; thereafter it becomes Grey and is called **Jupiter** or **Tin** or **Bismuth**, and that is before the Whiteness. After the Whiteness it is called **Mars** and **Venus** before it is brought to complete Redness. That **Basilius Valentinus** is of the same opinion and seeks something very different from what he describes in the aforementioned book, he testifies himself in his tract *Of the Great Stone*, where he investigates the *Material of the Stone* and says how in the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> the gift of all three fixities is together, and therefore it withstands all power of the fire: and that the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, because of its fixed **Mercury**, does not flee so quickly in the fire and passes its examination. Finally, he speaks: "The apprentice **Venus** <note>Copper</note> is clothed and occupied with superfluous color, and her master body is pure **Tincture** and

<column-break/>

just such a color as also dwells in the best metal and has superfluity: which proves itself upon red. And because her body is *drossy* <note>original: "Russischia," likely referring to the rust or "leprosy" of copper</note>, the constant **Tincture** can have no permanent dwelling, but must vanish together with her body. For where the body is consumed through killing <note>the alchemical process of "mortification" or breakdown</note>, the soul also cannot remain, but must give way and flee. For the dwelling is destroyed and burned with our fire, so that its place is not to be recognized, nor may any dwell there further. In a fixed body, however, it dwells gladly with understanding. The constant **Salt** has assigned and left to the combative **Mars** <note>Iron</note> a hard, severe, and coarse body, from which the bravery of its spirit is proven, and is not easily forced from this War-Prince, for his body is so hard that one cannot easily wound him." Now, if one wished to say that because **Venus** has a constant **Sulfur**, the same must be perfectly united with the **Spirit of Mercury** according to the teaching of **Basilius**, and a **Tincture** made therefrom: let him consider what has already been said many times and drawn from **Basilius** himself—that our material must not be taken from many things, because the **Universal** is a single thing, and is found and drawn out from a single thing at once; and let the **Spirit of Mercury** and the **Soul of Sulfur** together with the **Spiritual Salt** be united at once, enclosed under one sky, and dwelling in one body—then he will come out of such errors and turn his thoughts to the perfect metals without further reflection: considering the saying of **Plato**, Part 2, Chapter 4: "Why do you **Calcinate** and **Solve** the other bodies with great effort, seeing as you can find in these (perfect ones) what you seek? If you must ever use them, it is necessary that you first turn them into the nature of the perfect bodies."

Therefore, my dear researcher of natural secrets, leave all animal and vegetable things, all salts, alum, vitriol, marcasites, magnesias, antimony, all imperfect and impure metals, and seek your Stone with **Arnaldus de Villa Nova** (*The Rosary*, Part 1, Chapter 7) in **Mercury** and the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> for the gold work, and in **Mercury** and the **Moon** <note>Silver</note> for the silver work, seeing as the entire art is hidden herein. For just as fire is the beginning to make a fire, says **Ripley** (*The First Gate*), so also is Gold the beginning to make Gold. If you, therefore, wish to make Gold and Silver through the Philosophical art, then do not take for it eggs or blood, but rather Gold and Silver, which naturally and whiteness-wise <note>referring to the silver/white path</note>—but not with the hand—being **Calcinated**, bring forth a new birth, because its genus, like all other things, increases. Therefore **Richardus** <note>Richard the Englishman</note> admonishes in Chapter 10 that one should sow Gold and Silver, so that they may bring fruit with our labor and the mediation of nature: seeing as they have within them, and are that which one seeks, and no other thing in the world. And why should one not choose these two, since they have a pure and perfect **Mercury** and red and white **Sulfur** with them, as **Richardus** testifies in Chapter 12? For **Avicenna** says that in every gold there is a red **Sulfur**. Such **Sulfur**, however, is not found upon the earth except only in those two bodies. Therefore, we prepare these two bodies very subtly, so that we may have the **Sulfur** and **Mercury** from such material, from which Gold and Silver were made under the earth. For these are shining bodies, in which staining rays are hidden, which **Tinct** the other bodies into true red and white, after they have been prepared. For our mastery, as **Arnaldus** says in *The Rosary*, Book 1, Chapter 5, helps the perfect bodies and makes the imperfect ones perfect, without mixing any other thing. Because Gold then is the noblest metal, it is the **Tincture of Redness**, staining and transforming every body. Silver, however, is the **Tincture of Whiteness**, which stains all bodies into true whiteness.

But the good-hearted reader should be informed that the metals such as Gold and Silver in their metallic form are not the material of our Stone: they are the middle between our material and the perfect metals, just as our material is the middle between them and our **Great Stone**. Hear what **Bernard, Count of Treviso** says of this in the second part of his booklet: "Those who bring forth not our **Tincture**, but another which is not true, nor apparent, nor useful for any thing, may well remain silent: and those may also remain silent who say that there should be another **Sulfur** than ours, which is hidden in the belly of the **Magnesia**."

Also, those may well remain silent who wish to draw out another *Quicksilver* <note>original: "Argentum viuum"</note> than alone from the **Ferment** or the "Red Servant," and wish to draw out another Water than alone our ever-remaining one, which mixes itself with no other than alone that which is of its own nature, and softens or dissolves no other thing than alone that which is of its own metallic nature. For there is no other Vinegar but ours: no other Governance but ours: no other Colors but ours: no other **Sublimation** but ours: no other **Dissolution** but ours: no other **Putrefaction** but ours: no other material but ours.

Therefore, let go of Alum, Salt, Vitriol, and all other metallic salts, Borax, strong waters, and all herbs, animals, beasts and what comes from them—hair, blood, urine, human seed, flesh, eggs, and mineral stones, and every metal alone for itself. For although the entrance and our material are from them, and according to the aforementioned words of all Philosophers should be composed of **Mercury**, and such is found in no other thing than in the metals, as appears from **Geber**, etc.

Yet they are not our Stone so long as they are in their metallic form. For it is impossible that a single, indeed the same material, could have two forms at once. How then can they be the Stone, which has a worthy and middle form between the metal and **Mercury**, if they are not previously destroyed and the metallic form taken from them? Therefore speaks **Raymond Lull** in the 56th Chapter of his *Testament*: "Therefore the good artist takes the metals for the middle in the work of the mastery, and especially the **Sun** and the **Moon**, and that for the reason because both have come to a moderate equality and great purity of the sulfurous and mercurial essence, and are cooked, pure and well-ripened through nature's action, to which proportion the artist would torment himself in vain to reach if he wished to begin from the natural beginnings without active means to satisfy his search."

And further he speaks in his *Codicil*: "Without these two, namely Gold and Silver, this art cannot become perfect, because herein is the purest substance of **Sulfur**, which nature has perfectly cleansed; to reach which cleansing, Art is much weaker than Nature and cannot attain it, even if it exerts itself greatly."

From these two bodies, when they are prepared with their **Sulfur** or **Arsenic**, our **Medicine** can be made, but without them in no way. And in the preface to his *Key* <note>original: "Schlüffels"</note> he says: "I advise you, O my friends, that you work in nothing but the **Sun** and the **Moon**, bringing them back into their first material, namely into our **Sulfur** and **Mercury**." For from the bodies, says **Arnaldus** in *The Rosary*, Book 1, Chapter 7, the very white and red **Sulfur** is drawn out, because therein is most especially the purest sulfurous substance, conjured to the highest degree by nature.

<summary>This page argues that the Philosopher's Stone must be derived from Gold and Silver rather than base metals like Lead or Tin, because only the perfect metals contain the fixed, pure Mercury and Sulfur necessary for the Work. It clarifies that while the base metals are rejected, the "Earth" of Gold and Silver must be broken down and reborn to act as the true transmuting Tincture.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Sulfur, Geber, Basilius Valentinus, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Lull, Transmutation, Antimony, Tincture, Calcination, Gold, Silver, Saturn, Jupiter, Venus, Mars</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone. The previous page established that the Stone must have a metallic origin. Here, the author systematically rejects common minerals—such as Antimony, Zinc, Vitriol, and even common Mercury and Sulfur—as the true starting material, arguing that their impurities and "combustible" natures make them "enemies" to the work of transmutation.</meta>

Also, **Antimony** <note>original: "Antimonium"</note> does not only mix with metals, but naturally becomes **Lead** from them. Likewise, persons of both high and low status have seen many times that **Gold** was made from it. Since these substances are born from **Sulfur** and **Mercury**—into which they can also be reduced again through art—and share an origin with the metals, could the *Matter of the Stone* <note>original: "Materia des Steins"</note> be taken from them? To this I answer: first, that a distinction should rightly be maintained among these intermediate minerals; namely, between those that carry a Mercury within them, and those that do not. The first kind, which possess a Mercury, should rightly be considered, because their Mercury can also be transformed into Gold and Silver by our **Medicine** <note>In alchemy, the "Medicine" is another term for the Philosopher's Stone or its transformative elixirs.</note>. Therefore, in my opinion, they should be regarded as "half-metals" which have the disposition to become metals. The others, however, which have no Mercury, are worth nothing. But because of the evil and combustible sulfur found in them—which is the reason Antimony is an enemy to all metals and burns them, except for Gold, which it must leave in peace because of its stability—these substances cannot progress that far. One cannot choose them for the *Matter of our Stone*, which must be a pure, perfect Mercury and a pure, delicate, and incombustible Sulfur. That they are entirely impure and completely infected by their sulfur is easily seen when one "leads them to their school" and examines what they have learned <note>A metaphor for putting the substances through chemical testing and observation.</note>.

For one sees in **Zinc** <note>original: "Zincken"</note>—which one might take for pure Mercury based on its shine, weight, and outward appearance—that as soon as it enters the fire, it vanishes quickly in smoke like a pale yellow sulfur. **Marcasites** <note>A term often used for iron pyrites or other metallic-looking minerals</note> cannot be forced to melt with any flux because of their great earthy impurity. **Antimony**, however, which can be cleansed of its excessive blackness through clever maneuvers and brought into a very white and beautiful **Regulus** <term>Regulus: The metallic heart or "little king" of a mineral, obtained by smelting</term>, certainly looks as if something special could be made from it. Indeed, many who otherwise consider themselves wise are of the opinion that the Philosopher's Stone must be made from it. But! However much the Antimony is cleansed of its blackness, its bad nature and brittle sulfurousness remain with it. This is evident because it does not allow itself to be stretched under the hammer or become malleable, which is the property of every metal by which it is recognized alongside other traits.

Furthermore, it has a coarse, impure Mercury, not to mention that it always retains a volatile sulfur. And those who consider themselves great Philosophers—and wish to persuade others through many books and extensive writings that this very thing is the **Exemplar** <note>original: "Sempel"</note> of their **Universal** <note>The "Universal" refers to a medicine or solvent that works on all things.</note>—must kindly forgive me that I cannot accept their opinion. For one searches quite foolishly for something in a thing that is not there, as **Arnoldus** <note>Arnold of Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note> says; and because the "Short Way" <note>The alchemical "Short Way" or *via brevis* was a sought-after faster method of making the Stone.</note> consists of practice, the Philosopher's Stone is a pure matter. So also says **Lullius** <note>Ramon Llull, a major figure in medieval logic and alchemy</note> in his *Last Testament*: "Our Tincture is nothing but a pure fire." And in the *Vade Mecum* he says: "It is only the subtle spirit that tints and cleanses the bodies of their residue." But minerals, like others, are so coarse and impure that they cannot be brought away from it or internally purified in any way, unless it happens through the means of our **Tincture**. Thus, the *Matter of our Stone* cannot be obtained from them; since from nothing, as **Richardus** <note>Richardus Anglicus, a 13th-century alchemical author</note> says in Chapter 1, can anything be taken that is not already hidden within it.

But what shall one say of **Vitriol** <note>Metal sulfates, often green or blue</note>, which through its wonderful properties also leads many into error? Especially since a large part of it becomes **Copper**, and it can even transform **Iron** into Copper? Regarding this, it is briefly to be known that Vitriol is nothing other than a beginning and matter of Copper. In the veins of the earth, the mountain-vapors and misty Mercury encountered a place where such a bitter, constricting **Venusian Sulfur** <note>Sulfur associated with Copper ("Venus")</note> was found in such great quantity. The Mercury immediately accepted this, coagulated within it, and wished to become a metal. But because nature wished to separate the pure from the impure, the combustible from the incombustible, and the quantity of the aforementioned sulfur far exceeded the amount of Mercury, the Mercury had to be separated along with it and hide itself in the "Vitriolic Green."

This is clearly seen in that if one adds common sulfur—as the cause of this corruption—to copper and calcines it (for Art does with strong fire in a short time what Nature must perform with slow fire), it burns the copper entirely and brings it into the "Vitriolic state" through common maneuvers. And depending on whether there is much or little sulfur, the Vitriol becomes richer or poorer in color. From this it comes that one Vitriol has more copper-like properties than another; for example, in the **Cyprian** <note>Vitriol from Cyprus was traditionally blue copper sulfate</note> one finds much copper, in others less.

It is also well to note that the acidic spirit <note>Sulfuric acid</note> found in Vitriol stems from the sulfur, since one also finds and extracts it similarly from common sulfur. Thus, in the **Spirit of Vitriol** one also perceives the sulfurous smell, and the **Spirit of Sulfur** transforms the **Sulfur of Mars** <note>Iron</note> into a Vitriol, just like the Spirit of Vitriol does. Because there is also a coarse sulfur in Iron, the sharpness of the Vitriol eats it away, seeks its Mercury (which is not very unlike its own), and becomes good, malleable Copper through the union with its sulfur, which overcomes the Mercury.

Because there is such an abundant and rough sulfur in Vitriol, and so very little Mercury (which has also not yet reached its purification), we will gain no more from it than from the others. And we must heed the teaching of **Alphidius** <note>An early Arabic alchemical authority</note> in all these matters, who says: "My son, beware, and separate yourself from the dead bodies and stones, because there is no path to walk therein; since their life is not increased, but consumed. Such are the salts, **Orpiments** <note>original: "Auripigmenta," an arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, **Arsenic**, **Magnesia**, **Marcasite**, and the like."

And **Arnoldus** says in *Flos Florum*: "The cause of their error is because the four spirits—namely Orpiment, Sal ammoniac, Mercury, and Sulfur—are not the seed of either the perfect or imperfect metals; except for the Mercury and the Sulfur which coagulates the Mercury."

But someone might conclude from these last words of Arnoldus that common Sulfur and Mercury are the *Matter of our Stone*, because they are counted among the four spirits, and because sulfur coagulates Mercury? To this I must ask, with **Richardus** in Chapter 1: Does every sulfur coagulate Mercury? To that I answer, no. For every common sulfur, as the Philosopher says, is contrary to the metals. For it is to be known that sulfur is born from the "fatness of the earth" in the earth's cavities, and is made hard through moderate cooking, and then it is called sulfur.

However, there are two kinds of sulfur: living and combustible. The living is the active part in the metals, and when it is cleansed of all impurity by nature, it is the *Matter of our Stone*, about which more follows—

<column-break/>

The combustible and common kind, however, is not the Matter of the metals or the Stone <note>original: "lapidis"</note>, but their enemy. For common and combustible sulfur does not enter into our mastery, say **Avicenna** and **Richardus Anglicus**, because it did not spring from there; since it always infects, blackens, and corrupts, in whatever way it may be prepared by Art. For it is a destroying fire.

Therefore, it hinders fluidity when it is made "fixed." We see the example of this in **Iron**, which has a persistent, coarse, and impure sulfur within it. When that is burned, an earthy being comes from it, like a dead powder. How could this now give life to others? For it has a double excess: namely, that which can be ignited, and the earthy part.

From this, consider now the common sulfur, and not that of the Philosophers, which is a one-sided, living fire that makes other dead bodies alive and ripens them, etc. Therefore, common sulfur cannot be the *Matter of our Stone*. But what shall we say of common **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Quecksilber" or Mercury</note>? Since all Philosophers say that the *Matter of our Stone* is a Mercurial substance, and it has many properties attributed to our Quicksilver. For it is the beginning of all things that can be melted, as **Arnoldus** says in *Rosarium*, Book 1, Chapter 2: "For all meltable things, when they are melted, are turned into it, and it mixes with them because it is of their substance." Although the bodies are different in their composition from Quicksilver, depending on whether it has been left pure or impure by its foreign sulfur. And in Chapter 4 he says: "**Mercury vive** <note>Living or liquid mercury</note> is, in all its effects, clearly the most perfect and constant thing. For it protects against burning and causes flow when it is fixed, and is the **Tincture** to the redness, of abundant perfection, of shimmering light, and does not yield from the mixture as long as it lasts. It is friendly and sociable, and the means to join the tinctures together, because it allows itself to be mixed through and through with them and clings to them internally, since it is of their nature. It alone is that which overcomes the fire, but is not overcome by the fire, but rather rejoices in it and rests within it."

And **Bernhardus** <note>Bernhard Trevisanus, a 15th-century alchemist</note> says: "We follow nature most closely, which in its processes has no other material in which it works but the pure Mercurial form." In this Mercury lies the constant and incombustible sulfur, which completes our work without any other substance but the pure Mercurial substance. If there are such magnificent virtues in Mercury, must it not necessarily follow that it is the *Matter of our Stone*? To this we answer that, just as there are two kinds of sulfur, so also are there two kinds of Mercury: the common, and that of the Philosophers. Common Mercury is still a raw, immature, and "open" body, which does not remain in the fire like the Philosophical one, but is turned into smoke and chased away by gentle heat. Therefore, the Philosophers commonly say: "Our Quicksilver is not common Quicksilver." So also says **Lullius** in his *Clavicula*, Chapter 1: "We say that common Quicksilver cannot be the Quicksilver of the Philosophers, by whatever art it may be prepared. For common mercury cannot be kept in the fire, unless it happens through another bodily Quicksilver that is warm, dry, and more mature."

But the majority of Philosophers, in their abundance, have written about the **Sublimation** and other preparations of common Quicksilver. From this, many wonderful works have arisen, and the nature and property of this subject has been learned all the more; but the goal at which they aimed—namely, the great treasure of earthly wisdom—has never yet been found therein, because nature did not put it there. In truth, it looks so wondrous in its work that it might lead astray even one who calls himself wise. As an example: I knew a man who had amalgamated it with Gold and handled it so subtly that he brought it through all colors, even to **Citrinitas** <term>Citrinitas: The "yellowing" stage of the alchemical work, following the white stage</term>.

When, however, it would not yield from that color and he gave it a stronger fire—believing that it was now "fixed" <note>Fixed: rendered non-volatile or resistant to fire</note> and that, according to the Philosophers, one could not err with the fire in this way—the glass burst. The Mercury fled up the chimney, smeared the Gold with poison, and gilded the chimney itself. From which one nevertheless scraped together much Gold and reduced it again.

Thus it was seen that common Mercury, as a body itself, could neither open another body (namely Gold) nor work within it, even though many colors could be perceived therein because heat works upon moisture. Had this good man, like many others, undertaken what **Arnoldus** says in *Flos Florum*, he would not have proceeded in such a manner. For thus speaks Arnoldus, when he talks of such laborers: "As they looked at this more subtly, they considered that Quicksilver was the beginning and origin of metals; with sulfurous and boiling heat they sublimed the Mercury for themselves, thereafter they fixed it, they opened it and coagulated it, but when they came to the **Projection** <term>Projection: The final act of casting the Stone upon a base metal to transform it into gold</term>, they found nothing, etc."

Therefore, we also cannot regard common Quicksilver as the *Matter of our Stone*. We have hitherto sought the Matter of our Stone in animals, in vegetables, in stones, in the lesser minerals, and also in the intermediate and greater minerals, yet we have not found it. We must therefore look further to see if we can find it in the metals; and if it should be therein, whether it is in all of them at once, or only in some, and in which it is to be met. For it is known, and **Roger Bacon** <note>A 13th-century English philosopher and Franciscan friar</note> also testifies in his *Speculum*, Chapter 3, that all metals are born from sulfur and Quicksilver, and that nothing can be attached or added to them, nor anything transformed, except that which comes from them. Since every improvement increases the nature of the thing from which it is, as **Richardus** says in Chapter 1. It is also otherwise ordained in all of nature by the highest Creator that every thing brings forth and gives birth to its own kind, and that a horse is not born from a man. And just as irrational animals cannot bring forth their like for any increase unless it happens through the nature of their own kind: so you must not seek the true Stone—says **Basilius Valentinus** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist</note>—nor undertake to make it for yourself, except from its own seed, from which our Stone itself was made from the beginning.
