But to find this seed, you must consider within yourself why you desire to seek our Stone; thus it will become known to you that it must flow from nowhere else but a metallic root, from which the metals themselves were ordained by the Creator to be born. So there is also a great similarity in the birth of the metals and of the **Great Stone**, since in one and the other, the sulfur and Quicksilver—which have the **Salt** as the noble soul hidden within them—are necessarily required. And it is not possible to obtain the "salvation of utility" in metallic form until these three, taken from metallic beings, have been brought together into one; since nothing must be added to the metals that does not have its origin from them.

<summary>This page evaluates various minerals and common substances—Antimony, Zinc, Vitriol, and common Mercury—and rejects them as the "Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone due to their impurities. It concludes that the true "seed" of the Stone must be found within a purely metallic root, following the natural law that "like produces like."</summary>
<keywords>Antimony, Mercury, Sulfur, Vitriol, Zinc, Arnold of Villanova, Ramon Llull, Basilius Valentinus, Alchemical Matter, Metallic Seed, Projection, Citrinitas</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the "Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone. Having previously discarded common minerals and impure substances, the author now addresses whether imperfect metals (like lead, tin, or copper) can be used, ultimately concluding that while they contain the necessary principles, only the "perfect" metals—Gold and Silver—possess the purity required for the Great Work.</meta>

So it is obvious, as Bacchus <note>likely referring to pseudo-Bacchus, an alchemical authority cited in early modern texts</note> says, that no other thing, which does not take its origin from **Mercury** and **Sulfur**, is sufficient to make the Stone perfect or to bring about transformation. Therefore, it is necessary to take a **Metallic Matter** for the preparation of the Great Stone. However, whether such a thing is to be found in the **imperfect metals**, we wish to examine with a few words.

<margin>Geber, *Book of Furnaces*, chapter 9</margin>
Many are found who wish to have the *Matter of the Stone* <note>original: "Materiam Lapidis"</note> from **Tin** or **Lead** for the "white work" <note>the process of making silver</note>, and from **Copper** or **Iron** (or both) for the "red work" <note>the process of making gold</note>, doubtless misled by the Philosophers themselves. For thus speaks Geber in his *Book of Furnaces*, chapter 9: "The dough, which one must ferment, we draw, as is customary, from the **imperfect bodies** <note>the base metals</note>. And from this we give you a common rule: that the white dough is drawn from **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> and **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, but the red is drawn from **Venus** <note>Copper</note>, **Saturn**, and **Mars** <note>Iron</note>." So also teaches 
<margin>Basil Valentine in his book *On Natural and Supernatural Things*</margin>
**Basil Valentine** <note>a legendary 15th-century alchemist/monk</note> in his book *On Natural and Supernatural Things*, how to make a **Tincture** from the conjunction of **Mars** and **Venus**.

<margin>Basil Valentine in *The Triumphal Chariot [of Antimony]*</margin>
Likewise, in the *Triumphal Chariot*, he says: "Following this is the **Tincture of the Sun and Moon** <note>original: "Tincktura Solis & Lunae"</note>, etc., for the white; further, the **Tincture of Vitriol or Venus**, and likewise the **Tincture of Mars**, both of which also contain the **Tincture of the Sun** within them if they have previously been brought to a constant **fixation** <note>the process of making a substance stable in heat</note>. Following these are the **Tinctures of Jupiter and Saturn** for the coagulation of Mercury, and thereafter the **Tincture of Mercury** itself."

But the seeker of natural secrets should know that this was not the true meaning of Geber, Basil Valentine, or other Philosophers; otherwise, they would be speaking against themselves, which cannot be. For the Wise must never lie in their writings, even if they conceal the truth in **obscure speech**. For at once, no perfection can spring from the imperfect metals alone, nor when mixed together, by which they might be improved in the slightest. From themselves alone, such a thing cannot be, because the **purest essence of Mercury** is required for the Matter of our Stone, as *The Sound of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor Buccinae"</note>, Avicenna, Lull, and generally all Philosophers say: "We must choose the purest **Mercurial Substance** for our work." 

However, this purest substance of Mercury is not found by nature in the imperfect metals, because they are so corrupted and occupied by their foreign and impure sulfur that they are like **leprous bodies**. They cannot be brought to internal and perfect purification by any kind of art. Furthermore, they do not remain stable in the **fire**, which is a necessary property of our Matter—namely, that it remain constant in the fire—which does not happen here.

<margin>Geber, *The Sum of Perfection*, chapter 63</margin>
Let us hear what Geber says in his *Summa*, chapter 63, regarding this impurity of the imperfect metals and the property of perfect Mercury. Through this, by true discovery, we have found a wonderful class of two secrets: namely, that there are **three causes** for the destruction of every (imperfect) metal by fire. 

The first is that the **combustible sulfur** enclosed in their innermost essence is ignited by strong fire, diminishes the entire essence of the bodies, drives them into smoke, and finally consumes them, however good their quicksilver may be. 

The second cause is that the external flame is increased by them, passes through them, and dissolves them into smoke, whatever they may be. 

The third cause is that their bodies can be opened by **calcination** <note>reducing a metal to powder by heat</note>. For then the flame of the fire can pass through them and drive them into smoke, however perfect they might be. Now, when all causes of destruction come together, the bodies must necessarily perish and come to nothing. If, however, they are not together, the speed of the destruction of the bodies slows down somewhat. 

The other class of this secret is the **goodness** considered in the bodies because of the quicksilver. For quicksilver, because no causes of destruction or exhaustion are found in it, does not allow itself to be divided into parts of composition, but remains with its entire essence in the fire. From this, one must necessarily recognize the cause of its perfection. Therefore, let us praise the highly exalted and highest God, who created it and gave it that essence, and to the essence the property, which can be found in no other things in nature—that this perfection might be brought into it by any art—which we find in it in its **proximate potential** <note>original: "potentia propinqua"; meaning it is nearly ready to become the Stone</note>. For this is that which overcomes the fire and is not overcome by the fire, but rather rests in it in a friendly way and rejoices in it.

Through these recounted words, Geber proves without fail that the Matter of our Stone cannot be in the imperfect metals, because they are impure in themselves and flee entirely from the fire when one tries to purify them. Our Mercury, however, is constant in the fire because of its purity, and nothing can harm it.

Just as the imperfect metals alone are not the Matter of our Stone, they can even less produce it when mixed together, considering that through their mixing they become no purer than they were alone before.

Furthermore, a new **confusion** arises from this, which is entirely contrary to our intention, for only a **single matter** is required, as thought above. 
<margin>Haly, *Book of Secrets*, chapter 9</margin>
Haly testifies to this clearly when he says in the *Book of Secrets*, chapter 9: "It is one Stone, into which you should mix no other thing; with this the Wise work, and from this flows that which makes one healthy. Nothing else is mixed with it, neither in its whole nor in its parts."

<margin>Morienus; Arnold in *The Flower of Flowers*</margin>
And Morienus says: "This mastery originally reaches out from **one root**, which afterwards spreads into more parts and is turned back again into its thing." If, then, the imperfect metals cannot be the Matter of the Stone, why did the Philosophers command us to work in them? 

**Answer:** When the Philosophers commanded the taking of impure bodies, they did not mean Copper, Iron, Lead, Tin, etc., but rather understood **their Body** or **their Earth**, as Arnold says in *The Flower of Flowers*: "The Mercury is added to the Earth, that is, to the imperfect body." For although their Earth is in and of itself as perfect and pure as nature can make anything, it is still impure and imperfect with respect to the **Philosophical Stone**.

And herein Art surpasses Nature, because it performs that which Nature could not complete. But that this Earth is, as said, imperfect before perfect purification and **rebirth**, is apparent from the fact that it cannot yet **tinge** <note>to dye or transform other metals</note> or make perfect, and has nothing more than what Nature has bestowed upon it. When it is reborn, however, then it is capable of doing much. Its impurity, however, is clearly felt in our work, in that it becomes entirely **Black**, and is then compared to **Lead** or **Antimony**; after that, it becomes **Gray**, and is called **Jupiter** or **Tin** or **Bismuth**, and that is before the whiteness. After the Whiteness, it is called **Mars** and **Venus** before it is brought to complete Redness. That Basil Valentine is of the same opinion, and seeks something very different from what he describes in the aforementioned 
<margin>Basil Valentine in the treatise *On the Great Stone*</margin>
book, he himself testifies in his treatise *On the Great Stone*, where he investigates the *Matter of the Stone*. There he says how in the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> the gift of all three <note>Mercury, Sulfur, Salt</note> is together, and therefore it withstands all power of the fire. And that the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, because of its **fixed Mercury**, does not flee so quickly from the fire and withstands its examination. Finally, he says: "The protectress **Venus** is clothed and occupied with superfluous color, and her white body is pure tincture and

<column-break/>

just such a color as dwells in the best metal and has superfluity; this is proven by the red. And because her body is **leprous**, the constant **Tincture** cannot have a permanent dwelling, but must vanish along with her body. For where the body is consumed by killing <note>chemical destruction</note>, the **Soul** <note>the essence or tincture</note> also cannot remain, but must give way and flee. For the dwelling is destroyed and burned with fire, so that its place is not to be recognized, nor may any further dwell there. But in a **fixed body**, she dwells gladly with understanding. The constant **Salt** has assigned and left a hard, strict, and coarse body to the warlike **Mars**, upon which the bravery of his spirit is proven; and this War-Prince is not easily overcome, for his body is hard, so that one cannot easily wound him." 

But if someone wished to say that because Venus has a constant **Sulfur**, the same must, according to Basil's teaching, be perfectly united with the **Spirit of Mercury** and a Tincture made from it: let him consider what has already been said several times, and drawn from Basil himself—that our Matter must not be taken from many things, because the **Universal** is a single thing, and is found and drawn out of a single thing at once. And let the **Spirit of Mercury** and the **Soul of Sulfur** <note>original: "Anima Sulphuris"</note> together with the **Spiritual Salt** be joined together, enclosed under one sky and dwelling in one body; thus he will escape such errors and turn his thoughts to the **perfect metals** without further reflection, considering the saying of
<margin>Plato, *The Second of the Four*</margin>
**Plato** <note>referring to the *Platonis Quartorum*, a medieval alchemical text</note> in his second book: "Why do you calcine and dissolve the other bodies with great effort, since you can find in these (perfect ones) what you seek? If you must use them, it is necessary that you first turn them into the nature of the perfect bodies."

Therefore, my dear seeker of natural secrets, leave all animal and vegetable things, all salts, alum, vitriol, marcasites, magnesia, antimony, all imperfect and impure metals, 
<margin>Arnold of Villanova, *Rosary*, Part 1, ch. 7; Richard, ch. 10; Arnold, *Rosary*, Book 1, ch. 5</margin>
and seek your Stone with **Arnold of Villanova** in his *Rosary*, Part 1, chapter 7: in **Mercury and the Sun** for Gold, and in **Mercury and the Moon** for Silver, since the entire Art is hidden herein. For just as fire is the beginning for making a fire, says **Ripley** in his *First Gate*, so also is **Gold** the beginning for making Gold. 

Therefore, if you wish to make Gold and Silver through the Philosophical Art, do not take eggs or blood for it, but Gold and Silver, which—naturally and wisely, but not with the hand <note>meaning through chemical/philosophical means, not manual labor</note>—bring forth a new birth when calcined, because its kind, like all other things, multiplies. Therefore **Richard** also admonishes in chapter 10 that one should sow Gold and Silver, so that they may bring fruit with our labor and the mediation of nature: since they have it in them, and are that which one seeks, and no other thing in the world. And why should one not choose these two, since they have a pure and perfect Mercury and red and white sulfur with them, as Richard testifies in chapter 12? For Avicenna says that in every Gold there is a **red sulfur**. Such sulfur, however, is not found upon the earth except in these two bodies. 

Therefore, we prepare these two bodies very subtly, so that we may have the Sulfur and Mercury from such matter, from which Gold and Silver were made under the earth. For these are shining bodies, in which are hidden coloring rays, which tinge the other bodies into true red and white after they have been prepared. For our mastery, as Arnold says in the *Rosary*, Book 1, chapter 5, helps the perfect bodies and makes the imperfect ones perfect, without the mixing of any other thing. Because Gold is the noblest metal, it is the Tincture of redness, coloring and transforming every body. Silver, however, is the Tincture of whiteness, which colors all bodies into true Whiteness.

<margin>Bernard, Count of Trevisan</margin>
But the good-hearted reader should be informed that the metals, such as Gold and Silver in their **Metallic Form**, are NOT the Matter of our Stone. They are the **middle** <note>the intermediate state</note> between our Matter and the perfect metals, just as our Matter is the middle between them and our Great Stone. Hear what **Bernard, Count of Trevisan** and Neugen says about this in the second part of his booklet: "Those who bring forth not our Tincture, but another which is not true, nor apparent, nor useful for any thing, may well be silent: and those may also be silent who say that there should be another Sulfur than ours, which is hidden in the belly of the **Magnesia**."

Also, those may well be silent who wish to extract another **Argentum Vivum** <note>Quicksilver/Mercury</note> than from the **ferment** or the "red servant," and wish to draw another Water than only our **ever-lasting water**, which mixes with no other than that which is of its own nature, nor softens or dissolves any other thing than that which is of its own metallic nature. For there is no other Vinegar but ours; no other governance but ours; no other colors but ours; no other sublimation but ours; no other dissolution but ours; no other putrefaction but ours; no other Matter but ours.

Therefore, let go of alum, salt, vitriol, and all other minerals, borax, "strong waters" <note>acids like nitric acid</note>, and all herbs, animals, beasts, and what comes from them: hair, blood, urine, human seed, flesh, eggs, and mineral stones, and every metal alone for itself. For although the entrance and our Matter are from them, and according to the testimony of all Philosophers should be composed of **Quicksilver**, and such is found in no other thing than in the metals, as appears from Geber, etc.

Yet they are not our Stone as long as they are in their **Metallic Shape**. For it is impossible that a single matter can have two shapes at once. How then can they be the Stone, which has a worthy and intermediate form between the metal and Mercury, if they are not first destroyed and the Metallic Shape taken from them? Therefore 
<margin>Raymond Lull, chapter 56 of the *Testament*</margin>
**Raymond Lull** also speaks in the 56th chapter of his *Testament*: "Therefore the good artist takes the metals for the 'middle' in the work of the mastery, and especially the **Sun and Moon**; and that because both have come to a moderate equality and great purity of the sulfurous and mercurial essence, and are cooked, pure and well-ripened through the operation of nature, to which proportion the artist would struggle in vain to arrive if he wished to begin from natural beginnings without effective means to fulfill his purpose."

And further he speaks in his *Codicil*: "Without these two, namely Gold and Silver, this Art cannot be made perfect, because herein is the **purest substance of Sulfur**, which nature has perfectly purified; to which purification Art is much weaker than Nature and cannot attain it, though it troubles itself greatly for it."

From these two bodies, when they are prepared with their **Sulfur or Arsenic**, our **Medicine** can be made, but without them in no way. And in the preface to his *Key*, he says: "I advise you, O my friends, that you work in nothing but the Sun and Moon, bringing them back into their **First Matter**, namely into our Sulfur and Mercury." For from the bodies, says Arnold in the *Rosary*, Book 1, chapter 7, the very white and red 
<margin>Arnold, Book 1 of the *Rosary*, ch. 7</margin>
**Sulfur** is extracted, because in them most of all is the purest sulfurous substance, enchanted to the highest degree by Nature.

<summary>This page argues that the "Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone must be derived from the perfect metals, Gold and Silver, rather than base metals or common minerals. It emphasizes that while base metals are "leprous" and unstable in fire, Gold and Silver contain the purest sulfur and mercury necessary for transmutation once their metallic form is destroyed and reduced to their "first matter."</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Geber, Basil Valentine, Gold, Silver, Mercury, Sulfur, Tincture, Calcination, Arnold of Villanova, Raymond Lull, Bernard of Trevisan, Transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Matter" of the Stone. The author previously established that only Gold and Silver are suitable starting points. Now, the text distinguishes between "common" metals and the "living" metals of the philosophers, and begins the crucial explanation of "Solution"—the process of dissolving these metals into their primary state.</meta>

Thus speaks Nicaeus in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "turba [philosophorum]," a famous early alchemical text</note>: I command those followers who wish to multiply and renew gold to take it, then divide the Water into two parts. One part should be pressed together as the Gold itself: for the Ore, when it falls into this water, shall be called the Ferment of Gold. But why does the Philosopher here call the Water his Gold, when he says that when the Ore falls into this water, it shall be the Ferment of Gold? My art-seeking successor should know that the Philosophers' Gold is not common gold, as Senior <note>Zadith ben Hamuel, an 10th-century Arab alchemist</note> says and as is stated in the first exercise in the *Assembly*: just as **Mercury** is the beginning of all metals, so is the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> the end and last of the metals. All metals, whether pure or impure, contain within them the Sun, the Moon, and Mercury: but only one is the true Sun, which is drawn out from them.

Thus you understand that the Philosophers' Gold is a very different Sun or Gold than common gold, even if it was taken from it. So also says the *Rising Dawn* <note>original: "Aurora consurgens," a 15th-century manuscript</note> in chapter 16. From this it is obvious that Philosophical Gold is not common gold, neither in color nor in substance. Therefore, it is said that it gladdens the heart of man, and Silver does the same. But what is drawn out from them is a white and red, true, fixed, and living **Tincture**. This is the Philosophical Gold that one does not need to buy dearly, as Alphidius says: and Morienes <note>a legendary hermit-alchemist</note> speaks, saying every thing that is bought dearly is a lie. For with a little of this thing, and with a little Gold, we buy a great deal. But our Gold is **living Gold**, and our Silver is **living Silver**, which have nothing but life and effect. Common gold and silver are **Dead**; they can perform no more than nature has granted them until they are resurrected from the dead by the clever Artist and regain their life: then they live also, and can show great virtue in the multiplication and propagation of their kind. Of the death of common metals and the life of ours, the excellent and still living philosopher Michael Sendivogius <note>a famous Polish alchemist, 1566–1636</note> speaks thus in the 11th treatise of his booklet *On the Philosophical Stone*: "You should be warned not to take common gold and silver, for they are dead; take ours, which live. Thereafter, place them in our fire, and a dry moisture will come from them. First, dissolve the Earth in the Water, which is called the **Mercurius of the Philosophers**, and the Water dissolves those bodies of the **Sun and Moon** <note>Gold and Silver</note> and consumes them, so that no more than a tenth part remains with one part, and this is the deep-rooted moisture of the metals."

Now, to speak further of the Gold of the Philosophers, it must be known that the Philosophers sometimes call their Water "Gold," and sometimes their Earth. Of the first manner, the philosopher Nicarius spoke above, and the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Rosarius Philosophorum"</note> lets Gold be taken for Water. He adds here that the Philosophers say: "Our gold is not common gold, and our silver is not common silver." To this I say, they call the Water their "Gold" because it rises upward through the power of fire: and this Gold is truly not common gold. For the common man would not believe that it could rise to the top because of its stability.

But that the Philosophers also called their Earth "Gold" is likewise witnessed by this *Rosary*, where it says: "Know that the Ore is the Philosophers' Gold." This Earth is called ore, ferment, and tincture or soul, just as we have called their Water "white and foliated earth" and "water." And regarding this, the author who wrote *The Clanging of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangorem Buccinae"</note> says in the chapter on solution (as Hermes also speaks): "Sow your Gold in the white and foliated earth, which has been made fiery by calcination <note>heating to a powder</note>, subtle and airy; namely, sow as much Gold—that is, the soul and tinting power—into the white earth, which through proper preparation has become white and pure, in which there is no filth."

And from this it is obvious that the Gold of nature is not the matter of the ferment, but the Philosophers' Gold is the **tinting Ferment**. And in *The Ladder of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Scala Philosophorum"</note>, level 7, it says: "Their Earth, in which their Gold is sown, is white, and their Soul is Gold, and that same body is the place of Wisdom where she gathers, and the dwelling of the Tincture."

And a little further, this author speaks: "Therefore says Hercules; Pour back again, that is, dissolve the body of **Magnesia** <note>a mystical "first matter," not modern magnesium</note>, which has become white and like raspberry leaves. For it is that which flees into the best, and the Gold that is drawn out from it is called the Gold of the Philosophers, and is a Tincture, which is a Soul. For with the water, the Spirit rises into the higher air, and this white body, when the gold becomes white, but also they called a reddish soul among our Gold." Therefore Senior speaks: "Mix Gold with Gold, namely Gold and Earth." And Hermes: "Sow Gold in foliated earth." Therefore the Philosophers write that our gold is not common gold.

Someone might say and ask here regarding the above opinion: why do the Philosophers understand their Gold to be Water at one moment and Earth the next? That would seem as if they either contradict themselves and are not in agreement, or that they wish to confound and mislead their followers. To this the answer is: all and every Philosopher, when they showed the truth most openly, wrapped it again in hidden speech. They do not contradict one another, but agree wonderfully, as if they all spoke from one mouth. Thus they create no confusion and do not seek to mislead the worthy follower, but set before his eyes all their secrets clearly, yet in figurative speech. These they darken and hide from the unworthy and godless, as much as the highest God has granted them grace, so that such noble pearls are not cast before swine who seek only to follow their lusts, and so the sanctuary is not trampled underfoot: as the work itself proves in the present question.

For the good-hearted follower is sufficiently informed of our art, and it has been repeated many times, not only from where our **Matter** should be obtained, but also what the right Matter is, which through the Artist's wisdom is dissolved into two things, namely into Water and Earth, or Mercury and Sulfur. If the Philosophers now call the Water "Gold," or the Earth "Gold," they do no wrong, and it is within their free will to call it what they wish; especially since they call their Stone itself their Gold, a more than perfect gold, a reborn gold, and many other such names. That everyone cannot understand their meaning immediately is to be attributed more to their own ignorance than to the ill-will of the Philosophers.

And thus the art-seeking researcher of the over-precious secret has been abundantly and completely informed of the Matter of our great Stone: that it must not be drawn from any vegetable growths, from any animals or what is taken from them, from any mineral, from any imperfect metal, but from **Gold and Silver**—and that our Gold and our Silver are not common and dead Gold and Silver, but those of the Wise, which are living Gold and living Silver.

Now it remains that he also be instructed in our **Solution**, as one of the greatest secrets of the entire work.

<column-break/>

Now to speak of the **Solution** <note>the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note>: the same occurs when one makes a dry thing wet, a hard thing soft, and a hidden thing more manifest; that is, when one transforms a hard thing into water—not indeed into common water, as the philosophers Parmenides and Agadmoe teach in the *Assembly*, where they speak: "There are some who, when they hear of the dissolution of the bodies, imagine it is a water from the clouds. But if they had read and understood our books, they would know that our water is permanent." Rather, it is the Water of the Philosophers, that is, into the **First Matter** <note>the original chaotic substance from which all things grow</note>, as Arnold of Villanova says in the *Rosary*, book 1, chapter 9: "The work of the Philosophers is that they dissolve their Stone into their Mercury, that is, bring it back into its First Matter."

And Avicenna says: "If you wish to work, it is necessary for you to work first in the dissolution and sublimation of the **two lights** <note>the Sun/Gold and Moon/Silver</note>, since the first step of the work is that it becomes quicksilver." Therefore Arnold also describes the Solution in book 2, chapters 1 & 2, as a separation of the bodies and a preparation of the first matter or nature.

And Richard the Englishman <note>Richardus Anglicus, a 14th-century alchemist</note> says: "The beginning of our work is to dissolve the Stone into its First Matter, and it is the union of the body and spirit, so that a mercurial water comes from it." But as the Solution is the first and most necessary part of our work, so it is also perhaps the most difficult part of it, as Eubaldus Vogelius testifies not poorly when he says: "What a difficult work the Solution is, those who have labored in it can testify."

And John, Count of Treviso <note>Johannes Grafen von Tresne</note>, writes in his letter to Thomas of Bologna: "He who knows the art and science of dissolution has reached the secret of the art, which is to mix the forms and to draw out the natures from the natures which lie powerfully hidden within. For this Solution must not happen with 'sharp waters' <note>strong acids like nitric acid</note>, since all sharp waters destroy and ruin the Body which they are supposed to dissolve and bring to improvement." Furthermore, as has been said, no dissolution is required in water that wets the hands, but rather a **dry water**, which is the First Matter, which is called not only Mercury but also Sulfur. Unless you make an unfailing spirit, you labor in vain.

Zeumon speaks of this in the *Assembly* thus: "If you do not rub, break, moisten, and diligently govern the bodies until you draw out their fatness and make an unfailing spirit, you labor in vain."

And Richard the Englishman speaks by the instruction of Avicenna: "The Philosophers have diligently thought upon how they might draw the Sulfur out of the perfect bodies and better purify their properties through the Art, so that through the help of nature, that which was not seen in it before might come forth, although they held it perfectly hidden within them; and they said that this could never happen without the dissolution of the body into its First Matter." Let the body be moist again, which is then nothing other than quicksilver from which it was previously made, and this without any mixing or addition of a foreign thing. "For our bodies dissolve into no water except that which is of their kind, and which can give the bodies the same weight," says Bernhard <note>Bernard of Trevisan</note> in his letter to Thomas of Bologna. And a little before he speaks in the same letter: "The dissolution requires a joining together, namely of the dissolver and the dissolved, so that from both—namely the Male and Female names—a new form may arise. I tell you in all truth, no water dissolves the metallic form through natural reduction except that which remains with them in matter and form, and which the dissolved metals themselves can coagulate again."

And Morfoleus speaks in the *Assembly*: "Every body is dissolved with the spirit with which it is mixed, and it will undoubtedly happen with it, and every spirit is ended and colored by the bodies with which spirit a tinting and fire-stable color is mixed."

When all this is so, the art-loving disciple must diligently reflect on what this Water then is, since the knowledge of the **Menstruum** <note>a solvent, named after the monthly cycle to imply its rhythmic or generative nature in the "Great Work"</note>, as Raymond Lull says in *The Compendium of the Soul*, is one of those things without which nothing can be accomplished in the mastery of this Art. "For there is no thing in the world that joins the dissolution of metals together," he speaks in his *Codicil*, "but our Menstruum: since such is the water that is dissolved with the metals while maintaining their forms."

Because this is the great secret which the Philosophers have not only kept most hidden in all their writings but have also forbidden to reveal, I will nonetheless lead you, as much as is permitted to me, onto the right path with two Philosophical sayings. The first is found in the booklet *The Abbreviated Rosary* with the following words: "The first preparation and the foundation of the Art is the Solution, that is, the bringing back of the body into water, that is, quicksilver: and this they have called the Solution of the Hot, when they said: 'Let the gold that is hidden in the body of Magnesia be dissolved, so that it is brought back into its First Matter, so that Sulfur and Quicksilver come from it,' not that it be turned back into water which is not of the body's own nature." The third <note>perhaps referring to a third point in the cited text</note> however is the right one, and dissolved back into the nature of quicksilver, and the saltiness of its sulfur be lessened—which holy Sulfur is made, drawn from two sulfurs, when the spirit meets the body.

The other saying is taught by Ripley <note>George Ripley, 15th-century English alchemist</note> in the preface to his *Twelve Gates*: "I will teach you in truth, so that you understand, there are three Mercuries which are the keys of the science, and which Raymond called his 'Menstrua,' without which nothing right is made. Two of these are deviant, not being of the body's own nature. The third, however, is the essential Mercury of the Sun and the Moon, whose property I will explain to you. For the essential Mercury of the other metals is the foremost material of our Stone. In the Sun and Moon, our menstrua are not seen with eyes, but only through the effect."

This is our Stone, if anyone understands our writings rightly. It is the Soul and shining essence of the Sun and the Moon, and the very subtle influence through which the Earths receive their shine. "For what else is Gold and Silver," speaks Avicenna, "but a pure, white, and red earth? Take the aforementioned substance, and prepare it, and let the things of very little value be to you." When all is put together, we call it "Our Thing": the property of the shine stems from the Sun and the Moon: and these are, in total, our Menstrua.

The perfect bodies we calcine <note>reduce to powder by heat</note> naturally with the first: but no impure body comes to it, except one, which is commonly called the **Green Lion** <note>a symbol for a potent acidic solvent or a specific mineral like vitriol</note> by the Philosophers, which is the medium to join the tinctures between the Sun and the Moon with perfection.

With the second, which is a **Vegetable moisture** that makes living that which was previously dead, both material beginnings, as well as the formal ones, shall be dissolved; otherwise, they are of little value.

With the third, which is an altogether permanent moisture, incombustible and fatty in its nature, the **Tree of Hermes** <note>a symbol for the growth of the alchemical work</note> is burned into ashes. This is our certain natural Fire, our Mercury, Sulfur, our pure

<column-break/>

Tincture: our Soul, our Stone, which has been reborn by the Philosophers. Guard this well in your heart.

I may tell you that this Stone is our Sun, and our Sulfur; but how you find this Stone shall follow hereafter. For this Menstruum is in truth a lovely breeze and a Philosophical Water, when the elements are separated, and nature can appear as a clear water.

Out of and with this Menstruum, nature cannot go out; the natures are made when it is naturally calcined almost with its earth: then you can work with it.

These are now the words of these Philosophers, from which the Matter and the Solution are discovered. If you now hold all these sayings together and reflect, so that you might direct all your senses toward it—that is, bringing it back from the coarse and unwinding everything—you will find all truth therein. But you cannot yet notice much more from this if you do not know the Manual <note>the practical handbook</note>, and also the Matter, and the art of the Solution. Put on your glasses, or your "I thought so" <note>original: "vermeintes putabam," mocking those who assume they know without study</note>, and learn how one can be furthered in diligent study, alongside the contemplation of nature.

For after the revelation of the high God, I learned this art alone from diligent study and frequent experience. It is true that I learned the matter from it—not being able to learn from my own experience [alone]—but what I formed from the writings of the ancients, also the Solution, which in this work is the only thing, and without which neither the old nor the young can accomplish anything: hence they have this saying: *No philosopher [has accomplished this], because it is revealed to none except by God* <note>original: "Nemo Philosophorum, quod nemo nisi Deus est illis reuelata"</note>, which encompasses all work, for which I then give praise, honor, and glory to the kind God from my heart, now and in eternity, Amen.

So that you, dear reader, may have no burden from your toil and labor, I will, through the love of God who is the Truth, tell you whether a single Solution is enough; the Authors and the *post-things* <note>original: "posterius," likely referring to subsequent steps</note>, as one is accustomed to speak in the schools.

The first is the division, of which Arnold says in the *Assembly*: "Make the matter," as mentioned above. The other is the Calcination, of both the body and the spirit together, so that the volatile and the fixed always remain together, and this happens in the glass—the coagulation of the spirit. But the third Solution is, in my eyes, everything: all that you have to eat and drink, it is the whole "work of women" and "child's play" <note>common alchemical metaphors for the easy, final stages of the process</note>, without all the effort one has with it, of which you will in time be well informed, once you rightly know the beginning. To reach all this glory, so that we might see God in the dark, clarified songs of the Philosophers. Then we may despise all earthly lusts and, for the sake of this noble art, follow only our Lord God, from which very beautiful fruit will grow.

Herewith I will now conclude this short little treatise of what is necessary to know of our Stone, in the following **Parable**, and set it before your eyes, in which you may diligently follow the entire manner and way of the work; you will yourself reach the proposed goal of wisdom, to which may the kind God help you and us all:
->God, God the Son, and God the Holy Spirit,<-
->unto all eternity, Amen.<-

---

# Now follows the Parable, in which all three works are contained.

> There is a thing, single in number and essence,
> Which Nature, through the help of Art, has chosen,
> Into two, into three, four, five, as we do read,
> **Mercury** and **Sulfur** they call it,
> **Spirit, Soul, and Body**, they also confess,
> With the **Wise Stone** the fifth we name,
> Without deceit you should count this matter,
> Twofold: of mercurial substance without fail,
> Without foreign sulfur, you shall choose the form,
> And dissolve them well at the foundation,
> Weigh them according to the right weight,
> So they will lead you to Wisdom:
> After the Solution you should soon sublimate, and further,
> Also Calcine, and diligently distill,
> Coagulate and thereafter fix
> In one vessel, then proceed to the other,
> So you have Medicine for humans and metals
> To make healthy, according to all your pleasure.

As I once found myself in a beautiful, green pleasure garden and contemplated the hardships of this life—how man, through the many falls of our parents, has come into such misery and tribulation—I continued in such thoughts. And as I looked about me, I know not how, I stumbled onto a narrow footpath that was so impassable and overgrown with so many bushes that I easily recognized it had been very little trodden, and I was startled and would have gladly gone back. But it was not in my power, since a strong wind blew so mightily from behind me that I could sooner take ten steps forward than one back.

When I had gone on for a noble time, I came to a beautiful meadow, which was grown with many beautiful trees in a perfect circle and was called by the inhabitants the **Meadow of Happiness** <note>original: "Pratum Felicitatis"</note>. There was a crowd of old men, all with ice-gray beards, and among them was an old man with a pointed black beard whose name was indeed known to me, but whose face I could not see; he was younger, and they were disputing about Wisdom and about a high and great Secret, and how the Lord God kept it hidden from the world and revealed it to those who loved Him.

I listened to them for a long time, and their speech pleased me. Then some wanted to stray from the path, saying that they did not find the right Wisdom but only Parables, similarities, and enigmas. In this they followed Aristotle, Plato, and others, who had each copied from the other. I did not want to hold with that, but mixed my own opinion in with theirs, wanting things from Experience, and would not let myself be fooled.

<summary>This page explores the distinction between common metals and "Philosophical Gold," emphasizing that the alchemical matter must be "living" and derived from Gold and Silver. It introduces the critical stage of "Solution," the process of returning metals to their liquid "First Matter" using a "dry water" or "menstruum."</summary>
<keywords>Philosophical Gold, Living Gold, Solution, First Matter, Menstruum, Three Mercuries, Green Lion, Calcination, Parable of the Garden, Pratum Felicitatis, Sendivogius, Ripley, Arnold of Villanova</keywords>

<meta>This page continues an allegorical narrative of alchemical initiation. The narrator, having previously discussed the "Matter" of the Stone, now enters a trial or examination by a group of elders (the "Faculty"). The "Lion" mentioned here is a common alchemical symbol for the raw, corrosive mineral or mercury that must be "tamed" or processed.</meta>

...me in their faculty, they put me through the fire <note>The German "durch die Brände" implies a trial by fire or a rigorous examination</note>, but my foundation was so good that I passed with all honors, at which they all wondered together, and they unanimously decided to receive and accept me into their college, in which I then rejoiced from my heart.

But, they said, I could not yet be a true colleague until I first truly got to know their lion, and perfectly knew what he could do and achieve internally as well as externally. Therefore, I should apply myself to make him submissive to me. I trusted myself quite well and promised them I would do my best: for their company pleased me so well that I would not have taken a great deal of money to be parted from them.

They led me to the lion and described him to me very diligently: but how I should handle him at first, no one would tell me. Some among them did give hints, but so <term>confusedly</term> <note>original: *confuse*</note> that one in a thousand could not understand them. But once I had secured him and protected myself from his sharp claws and pointed teeth, they withheld nothing further from me. Now the lion was very old, fierce, and large; his yellow mane hung over his neck, and he seemed quite invincible, so that I was half-terrified by my own temerity and would gladly have turned back, had my promise and the fact that the old ones stood around me, waiting to see what I would begin, not held me back. I stepped toward the lion into his pit with good confidence and began to flatter him; but he looked at me with his bright, shining eyes so intensely that for fear I nearly lost my courage <note>original: "das für Furcht mir bey nahe das Wasser über die Körbe gangen wäre" - a colorful idiom for being overwhelmed by fear</note>. Nevertheless, I remembered what I had heard from one of the elders as we went toward the lion’s pit: that many people had undertaken to subdue the lion, but few were able to bring it to an end. If I did not want to be put to shame, and remembering many a trick I had learned through great diligence in this <term>athletic contest</term> <note>original: *Athletica*</note>, and being also well experienced in natural <term>magic</term> <note>original: *magia*</note>, I forgot the caressing and attacked the lion so nimbly, skillfully, and subtly that before he truly noticed, I drew the blood out of his body—yes, even out of his heart. It was a beautiful red, but very <term>choleric</term> <note>original: *choleriſch*; in the four humors, this signifies a hot, dry, and fiery nature</note>. I dissected further and found things that made me wonder: especially his bones, which were as white as snow, and there were many more of them than of his blood.

When my dear elders, who stood above around the pit watching me, realized this, they disputed heatedly among themselves, as far as I could gather from their movements. But what they said, I—being deep down in the pit—could not hear. Yet when they came to hard words with one another, I heard one say: "He must also make him alive again, otherwise he cannot be our colleague." Not wishing to make much fuss, I left the pit across a large square and came, I know not how, onto a very high wall that rose over 100 cubits <note>An "Elle" or cubit was a standard unit of length, roughly the length of an arm</note> toward the clouds. At the top, however, it was not even a foot wide, and from the beginning where I climbed up to the end, a bronze handle ran right through the middle of the wall, secured with many cast supports. On this wall I came, I say, and it seemed to me that someone was walking several steps ahead of me on the right side of the handle.

But after I had followed him for a while, I saw someone following behind me on the other side as well—I still doubt whether it was a man or a woman—who called to me and said it was better to walk on their side than where I was going. I easily believed this, for because of the handle standing in the middle, making the path very narrow, it was very difficult walking at such a height. For I also saw several who wanted to go that way fall down behind me. Therefore, I swung myself under the handle, holding on very firmly with my hands, and thus proceeded on the other side until I finally came to a place on the wall that was very steep and dangerous going down. Then I first regretted that I had not stayed on the other side, and I could no longer get back underneath to that side, nor was it possible for me to turn around and set out on the other path. Therefore, I took the risk, trusted my good feet, held on tight, and came down without harm; and when I had wandered a little further, I saw and knew of no more danger, nor did I know where the wall or the handle had gone.

After I had come down, there stood a beautiful rosebush on which beautiful red and white roses grew, but many more red than white. I plucked several of them and stuck them in my hat. But then it seemed to me there was a wall going around a large garden; in the garden were young fellows, and the maidens would gladly have been in the garden with them, but they did not want to wander far or apply much effort to reach the doors. I took pity on them and went back the way I had come, but on level ground, and went so quickly that I soon came to several houses where I expected to find the gardener’s house. But I found very many people there; each had his own chamber, and they were working diligently, usually two together, but each had his own work. What they were doing, it seemed to me, I had also done and worked at before, and all their work was known to me. I thought especially: "Look, so many other people also do such filthy and sloppy work that has only an appearance according to each one's imagination, but has no foundation in nature; so you are also to be forgiven." Therefore, because I knew that such arts vanish with the smoke, I did not want to delay there in vain any longer and continued on my intended way.

As I went toward the garden door, some looked at me sourly from one side, so that I feared they would hinder me in my <term>purpose</term> <note>original: *Propoſito*</note>. But others said: "Look, he wants to go into the garden, and we have performed garden service here for so long and have never gotten in; how we will laugh at him when he fails." But I paid no mind to all of this because I knew the opportunity of this garden better than they, even though I had never been inside. Instead, I went straight to a door that was firmly locked, so that from the outside one could not see or find a keyhole. But I noticed a small round hole in the door that could not be seen with common eyes, and immediately thought the door must be opened there. Therefore, I was ready with my specially prepared <term>lock-pick</term> <note>original: *Diederich*</note>, unlocked it, and went inside. Once I was through the door, I found several other bolted gates, which I opened without effort. This was a passage, as if in a well-built house, about six feet wide and twenty long, with a floor above. And although the other doors were still closed, once the first door was opened, I could see sufficiently into the garden.

In the name of God, I wandered further into the garden, where I found in the middle a small garden, square in shape, about six rods <note>A "Meßrute" was a surveying rod, about 10-12 feet</note> long on each side. It was hedged with rose thorns, and the roses bloomed very beautifully. Because it was raining a little and the sun was shining, it caused and produced a very lovely rainbow. When I had passed by that little garden and wanted to go to the place where I was to help the maidens, behold, I noticed that instead of the wall, a low woven fence stood there, and the most beautiful maiden, adorned in entirely white <term>satin</term> <note>original: *Atlaß*</note>, went with the stateliest youth, clothed below in <term>scarlet</term> <note>original: *Scharlacken*</note>...

<summary>The narrator undergoes an alchemical initiation involving the slaying and dissection of a "Lion" to obtain its red blood and white bones. Following this, he navigates a narrow, dangerous wall and gains entry into a secret garden containing red and white roses.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemical initiation, Green Lion, Red Lion, Garden of the Philosophers, Red and White Roses, Alchemical allegory, transmutation stages</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrator's journey after he has gained the favor of the "Faculty" and faced the lion. The narrative shifts from the trial of the lion to the central alchemical allegory: the "Chymical Wedding" or the union, death, and resurrection of the royal pair (the King and Queen), representing the stages of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

He passed by the Rose Garden <note>The Rose Garden is a common alchemical symbol for the "Great Work" or the location where the transformation of matter takes place</note>, one leading the other by the arm, and carrying many fragrant roses in their hands. I spoke to them and asked how they were able to get over the fence. "This, my most beloved bridegroom," she said, "helped me over, and we are now going out of this lovely garden into our chamber to enjoy our friendship." "I am glad," I said, "that your will is satisfied without further effort from me. Nevertheless, you see how much I have hurried, having run such a long way in so short a time to serve you." Afterward, I came into a large mill, built of stone inside; there were no meal-boxes or other things belonging to grinding, but one saw through the walls several water-wheels turning in the water. I asked what the occasion for this was, and an old miller answered me that the grinding-works were closed on the other side. Then I saw a miller's apprentice go in by the sluice-gate bridge, and I followed him. When I had come over the bridge, which had the water-wheels on the left hand, I stood still and wondered at what I saw there. For the wheels were now above the bridge, and the water was coal-black, though its drops were white; and the sluice-bridge was not more than three fingers wide. Nevertheless, I ventured back again, holding onto the timbers that were over the sluice-bridge, and so I came safely and without accident across the water. Then I asked the old miller how many water-wheels he had. "Ten," he answered. This adventure stuck in my head, and I would have liked to know what the meaning was. But when I perceived that the miller did not want to speak further, I went away. In front of the mill was a raised, paved hill; upon it were several of the aforementioned elders walking in the sun, which was then shining very warmly. They had a letter written to them by the whole Faculty, and they were consulting over it. I soon perceived what the content might be, and that it concerned me; therefore I went to them and spoke: "Gentlemen, is this about me?" "Yes," they said, "you must keep the wife you recently took in marriage, or we must report it to our Prince." I said, "That requires no effort, for I was as if born with her and raised with her from childhood; and since I have taken her once, I will also keep her forever, and death itself shall not part us: for I love her from a fervent heart." "What complaint have we then?" they answered. "The bride is also satisfied, and we have her consent; you must allow yourselves to be <term>joined</term> <note>original: *copuliren*; in alchemy, this refers to the chemical union of substances</note>." "Most gladly," I said. "Well then," said one, "so shall the lion receive his life again, and become much more powerful and stronger than before."

Then my previous toil and labor came to mind, and I thought to myself, for special reasons, that it must not concern me, but another whom I knew well. In that moment, I saw our bridegroom with his bride walking along in their previous <term>garb</term> <note>original: *Habit*</note>, ready and prepared for the wedding, at which I greatly rejoiced: for I had been in great fear that the matter might concern me personally.

When now, as mentioned, our bridegroom in his shimmering scarlet clothes, with his dearest bride—whose white satin dress gave off very bright rays—came to the said elders, they joined the two of them immediately. I wondered not a little that this maiden, who was supposed to be her bridegroom's mother, was still so young that she seemed to have only just been born.

Now, I do not know what sin these two must have committed, except that, because they were brother and sister <note>The "incestuous" union of brother and sister is a standard alchemical metaphor for joining two substances derived from the same primary matter</note>, they were so bound together with love that they could not be brought apart again, and thus might be accused of incest. These two, instead of a bridal bed and a magnificent wedding, were condemned and locked into a permanent and everlasting prison. Because of their high birth and distinguished rank, and so that they should commit nothing secret in the future, but all their actions should be known and visible to the watch appointed over them, the prison was formed to be entirely transparent, bright and clear, like a crystal, and round like a heavenly sphere. There they were to do penance and make amends for their committed misdeeds with constant tears and true repentance. But first, all foreign clothing and jewelry they had put on for adornment were taken from them, so that they had to dwell together in such a chamber entirely naked and bare. No one was given to them who might have gone into the chamber to wait on them; rather, after all the necessary food and drink—which was drawn from the aforementioned water—had been placed inside, the door of the chamber was very firmly barred and locked, and the seal of the Faculty was pressed upon it. I was commanded to guard them in this, and since winter was at the door, I was to warm the chamber properly so that they might neither freeze nor burn, and that they might not come out or escape by any means. But should any harm occur through neglect of this mandate, I would justly be subject to great and heavy punishment. I was not at ease with the matter; my fear and anxiety made me faint-hearted. For I thought to myself that what had been commanded of me was no small thing; also, I knew that the *College of Wisdom* did not lie, but what it said, it certainly carried out. However, because I could not change it, and moreover, since this locked chamber stood in the middle of a strong tower surrounded by strong bulwarks and high walls, in which the whole chamber could be warmed with a moderate but steady fire, I undertook this office and began in God's name to warm the chamber and to protect the imprisoned couple from the cold. But what happens? As soon as they felt the slightest warmth, they embraced each other so lovingly that the like will not soon be seen; they remained in such fervor that the young bridegroom's heart melted in his body for fervent love, and his whole body dissolved in his beloved's arms and fell apart. When she, who loved him no less than he loved her, saw this, she wept for him magnificently with her tears and, as it were, buried him, so that because of her shed tears, which flooded everything, one could no longer see where he had gone. She carried on this weeping and mourning for a short time, and for great heartache did not want to live any longer, but voluntarily gave herself over to death. Oh, woe is me! In what fear, distress, and anxiety was I, that I should see both entrusted to me as if entirely dissolved into water and lying dead before me. My certain downfall stood before my eyes, and what was most burdensome to me, I feared the scorn and mockery hovering before my eyes more than the damage that might befall me.

As I had now spent several days in such anxious thoughts, and deliberated back and forth how I might advise my affairs, it finally occurred to me how Medea had made the dead body of Aeson living again <note>A reference to the Greek myth where the sorceress Medea restores youth to her father-in-law Aeson by boiling him in a cauldron with magical herbs—a metaphor for alchemical rejuvenation</note>. I thought to myself: if Medea could do such a thing, why should it fail for you? I began then to consider how I should do it; but I found no better way than to persist with steady warmth until the waters had vanished and I might see the dead bodies of our lovers again; then I hoped to escape all danger with great benefit and praise. Therefore, I continued with the warmth I had begun, and continued the same for forty whole days, when I

<column-break/>

became aware that the water decreased more and more, and the dead bodies, which were as black as a coal <note>This is the *Nigredo* or "blackness" stage, representing the putrefaction and death of the old matter</note>, began to let themselves be seen again. Indeed, this would have happened sooner if the chamber had not been so firmly locked and sealed. This I dared not open by any means. For I noticed quite clearly that the water, which rose on high and hastened toward the clouds, gathered together again at the top of the chamber and fell down like rain; so that nothing could escape from it, until our bridegroom with his dearest bride lay dead and rotted before my eyes, stinking beyond all measure. Meanwhile, in the chamber, an exceedingly beautiful rainbow <note>The *Cauda Pavonis* or "Peacock’s Tail," signifying that the diverse colors of the work are appearing</note> was seen from the sunshine in the moist weather, with exceedingly beautiful colors, which gladdened me not a little after my endured sorrow; but I was much more joyful that I saw my two lovers lying before me again. But as no joy is so great that it is not mixed with much sadness, so I was also troubled in my joy because my charges still lay dead before me, and one could perceive no life in them. But because I knew that their chamber was made of such pure and thick material, and so firmly locked, that their soul and spirit could not get out, but were still safely kept inside, I continued with my steady warmth, day and night, to perform my commanded office, fully imagining that the two would not return to the bodies as long as the moisture lasted. For in moist nature, they like to remain. As I then found in deed and truth. For through diligent watching, I became aware that from the earth toward evening, by the power of the sun, many vapors rose up and drew themselves on high, just as if the sun were sucking water. These <term>coagulated</term> throughout the night into a lovely and very fertile dew, which fell down very early in the morning and moistened the ground, and also washed our dead bodies, so that from day to day, the more such bathing and washing occurred, the longer they became more beautiful and whiter <note>The *Albedo* or "whiteness" stage, representing purification</note>. The more beautiful and whiter they became, the more the moisture vanished, until finally, when the air was bright and beautiful, and all foggy and moist weather was over, the spirit and soul of the bride could no longer contain themselves in the bright air, but entered again into the <term>clarified</term> <note>original: *Clarificirten*; purified or light-filled</note> and now transfigured body of the Queen, who immediately felt the same and became alive again in that instant. This, as you can easily imagine, gladdened me not a little, especially when I saw her stand up, adorned in an exceedingly precious garment, the like of which has been seen by very few on earth, and with a precious crown of pure diamonds, and hear her speak thus: "Listen, you children of men, and take heed, you who are born of women, that the Most High has power to set up kings and to depose kings: He makes rich and poor according to His will: He kills and makes alive again.

See in me a true and living example of all this: I was great, and became small; but now I am old, I having been humbled, some kings raised above many kingdoms: I have been broken on the wheel and made alive again; we poor ones are entrusted and given the great treasures of the wise and powerful.

Therefore, the power has also been given to me to make the poor rich, to grant grace to the humble, and to bring health to the sick. But I am not yet like my most beloved brother, the great and mighty King, who is yet to be raised again from the dead: when he comes, he will prove that my words are true."

And when she had said this, the sun shone very brightly, and the day became warmer than before, and the <term>Dog Days</term> <note>The hottest days of summer, representing the intense heat of the final alchemical stage</note> were at the door. Because long before, for the magnificent and great wedding of our new Queen, many kinds of precious robes had been prepared—such as of black velvet, ash-colored damask, gray silk, silver-colored taffeta, snow-white satin, yes, an exceedingly beautiful piece of silver cloth embroidered with precious pearls and magnificent bright-glittering diamonds—so also, in like manner, different clothes were prepared and made ready for the young King: namely, of incarnat <note>a flesh-colored or crimson red</note>, yellow orange colors, precious fabric, and finally a red velvet garment embroidered with a very great quantity of precious rubies and carbuncles. The tailors, however, who made such clothes, were entirely invisible, so that I even wondered, when I saw one robe after another and one garment after another being finished, how such a thing could have happened, since I well knew that no one but the bridegroom with his bride had gone into the chamber. But what wondered me most of all was that as soon as another robe or garment was finished, the previous ones vanished before my eyes, so that I did not know where they went or who had locked them away.

When now this precious garment was finished, the great and mighty King also appeared, in great splendor and glory, to which nothing can be compared. And when he found himself locked in, he asked me kindly and with very gracious words that I should open the door for him and allow him to come out; it would be to my great benefit. Although it had been strictly forbidden for me to open the chamber, the great appearance and the lovely eloquence of the King so terrified me that I willingly opened it for him. And when he went out, he was so friendly, so gracious, yes, so humble, that he proved in deed that nothing so well adorns high persons as these virtues.

But because he had spent the Dog Days in great heat, he was very thirsty from exhaustion and fatigue, and commanded me that I should draw and bring some of the running, fast water from under the mill-wheels; which, when I had performed it, he drank a large portion with great desire. He went back into his chamber and commanded me to lock the door firmly behind him, so that no one might disturb him or wake him from sleep.

Herein he rested a few days and called to me to open the door. It seemed to me that he had become much more beautiful, full-blooded, and magnificent, which he then also noticed and considered it to be a magnificent and healthy water; he also immediately demanded more water and drank much of it, more than before, so that I finally decided to build the chamber much larger. When now this King had drunk enough of this precious drink—which the ignorant nevertheless value as nothing—according to his own will, he became so beautiful and magnificent that in my whole life I have seen neither a more magnificent person nor a more magnificent manner and being. Then he led me into his kingdom and showed me all the treasures and might of the world, so that I must confess that not only did the Queen proclaim the truth, but also left the greater part to those who know him. For of gold and noble carbuncle stones there was no end; rejuvenation and restoration of natural forces, as well as the return of lost health and the removal of all diseases, was a common thing there. But that was the most precious of all: that the people of that land know, fear, and honor their Creator, and obtain from Him wisdom and understanding, and finally, after this temporal glory, eternal salvation. To which may God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit help us.
->A M E N.<-

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process through the allegory of a Royal Wedding, where a King and Queen are sealed in a glass vessel, undergo a symbolic death (dissolution), and are resurrected through steady heat and purification. This represents the stages of Nigredo, Albedo, and finally Rubedo, resulting in the creation of the Philosopher's Stone and the attainment of ultimate wisdom and health.</summary>

### And by His wounds we are healed.
## ENTER IN. <note>original Latin: "INGREDERE"</note>
#### by the Life of LIGHT. <note>original Latin: "per Vitam LUCIS"</note>

<column-break/>

To him who overcomes, I will give to eat of the hidden manna, and I will give him a white stone, and on the stone a new name written, which no one knows except him who receives it. Revelation 2:17.

Christ is the stem and tree of life, through which the bitter water at Marah <note>Refers to the biblical story in Exodus 15 where Moses sweetens bitter water with a piece of wood.</note> is made sweet, and we are His branches and limbs, in and through which He brings forth fruit by His power in us. He has reborn that which had faded from the bitterness of this temporal life back into the sweetness of eternal life.

His soul is the proper character of my soul, and it was for the sake of the soul that God became man, so that we, in and through Him, might be "deified" <note>The term "vergöttet" refers to *theosis* or divinization—the mystical idea of the soul being transformed into a god-like state through union with Christ.</note> again in the love and life of Jesus Christ, and be reborn and revealed as an eternal heavenly image, a divine life. O man, it is a great, unfathomable mystery when we rightly consider and remember that we are members of one body in Christ, and in Him we are all only One. That we all enjoy and receive the one Christ, and in that same Christ, we are and remain only one single body—which He Himself is—within His members. Indeed, we are joined and bound with and in Him into a single man, and that same single man is every individual in Christ Himself. We all eat and drink Christ's flesh and blood under bread and wine (as through a medium) with the essential desire and the hungry mouth of faith—understand this as the fiery mouth of love and life within the soul. The true inner MAN, who is a being of Paradise and eternity and God’s image, hidden beneath the animal coarseness of the body, desires a spiritual <term>nutriment</term> <note>nourishment</note> or <term>tincture</term> <note>In alchemical and mystical terms, a "tincture" is a spiritual essence that can penetrate and transform matter.</note> (for he is himself a spiritual being) and eats Christ’s spiritual flesh and blood.

The soul’s hunger of faith is the divine spiritual mouth that eats and drinks Christ’s flesh and blood in the inward ground of the mind. That which proceeds from the Spirit and consists in the Spirit is the highest and greatest nourishment for the soul. The spiritual, hungry soul-fire grasps, seizes, eats, and drinks in its spiritual mouth of faith—opened in full desire—the holy, incomprehensible being of Christ’s flesh and blood. Into the soul's mouth comes the supernatural, holy, essential power under bread and wine, as an ordained means through which the invisible Eternal gives itself to the visible, spiritual, immortal, eternal man. Understand that in such a partaking, faith becomes a "being," like a spiritual flesh and blood. For one faith catches the other: a living, earnest faith catches the Eternal, and is so mighty and powerful that it can overturn and move mountains. For the true, essential faith in man is Christ Himself, who is and remains in him, and is his life and light.

See, thus we are purified through faith and illumined and enlightened through the Holy Spirit. The coarse animal, which is only a shell, receives only an elementary being or food, and eats from the perishable essence of the world from which it came; but the soul eats from God and His Word, from which she came. Note: as the mouth is, so is the food. Each <term>principle</term> <note>A foundational reality or realm of existence.</note> eats and drinks of its own likeness. Every spirit eats and drinks of that from which it originated, and in whose ground it stands within its center. For what fellowship has light with darkness? Darkness cannot comprehend it.

The natural, mortal, and corruptible body receives its nourishment from the earth; and the <term>sidereal</term> <note>Star-driven or astral; the "body" influenced by the heavens in Paracelsian thought.</note>, fleeting, and perishable body receives its nourishment from the firmament; but the Spirit of the Lord is the life of the soul, and the Life of the inner immortal life.

The inner man, as the pure Adamic power-body, together with his beautiful, chosen, gracious, lovely, heavenly bride, receives into his soul-mouth of faith the invisible being and supernatural heavenly flesh of Christ with great desire of love—a tincture of life, a penetrating fiery essence of love and power. For faith is the foundation, the author, and the finisher; it works the true inward everlasting Sabbath, the power of God, through the love of Jesus, in quiet rest and gentle bliss in the inner ground of the soul within us.

Christ did not give His disciples the creaturely, external, comprehensible, fleshly humanity or the formed being; no, whoever does not distinguish the Lord's body from the bread and wine receives it to his own judgment. He gave them the spiritual humanity, the powers of His body and blood, a holy, heavenly beauty and loveliness of the body, a spiritual being, a spiritual body which He brought from heaven, in which the divine and eternal human power is understood. This He introduces into our body, which has faded from the light, so that He may make it alive through His heavenly essence within Him.

No perishable creature can behold a spiritual being, much less can we grasp, seize, and enjoy such a thing with our earthly, mortal mouths. But to the spirit of the soul, which stands in the divine center, it is graspable and comprehensible, for He, Christ, is the body of the spirit and the spirit of the body—our soul’s body, which He gives us to eat in faith. No mortal or unworthy person can or will receive or attain, much less taste, the unchangeable and incomprehensible Bread of Heaven of eternity.

It consists of ten circles (Sephiroth) representing the attributes of God, arranged in three vertical columns and connected by lines (paths). At the top of the central axis is a Mercury symbol, often used in alchemy to represent the spirit or the mediator. The circles are labeled with traditional names: Crown (Kether), Wisdom (Chokmah), Understanding (Binah), Mercy (Chesed), Strength or Severity (Geburah), Beauty (Tiphereth), Victory (Netzach), Splendor (Hod), Foundation (Yesod), and Kingdom (Malkuth). Various divine attributes are written in or next to the circles, such as "Divine Eye" <note>original Latin: "Oculus Divinus"</note>, "Compassion" <note>original Latin: "Misericordia"</note>, and "Justice" <note>original Latin: "Justitia"</note>.
